# RS485 (Modbus RTU) → MQTT Sparkplug B gateway — ESP-IDF implementation

This is the MVP firmware (`docs/SPEC.md` §5) for the Waveshare **ESP32-S3-POE-ETH-8DI-8DO**.

- **Stack:** ESP-IDF **v6.1**, target `esp32s3`, the common configuration schema of §6.
- **Feasibility study for all of §4:** `docs/feasibility-idf.md`.
- **Connect the RS485 bus** (termination, bias, topology, error counters): `docs/how-to/connect-the-rs485-bus.md`.
- **Get and decode a core dump** after a crash (web API, shell, `esp-coredump`): `docs/how-to/decode-a-core-dump.md`.
- **Use the gateway with UMH Core:** `docs/how-to/connect-to-umh-core.md` (procedure), `docs/explanation/sparkplug-names-and-the-uns.md` (the name rules) and `docs/reference/umh-names-and-topics.md` (name limits and UMH topics).

> **Warning: Do not use these results as hardware results. Nobody tested the firmware on hardware.** No board was available. Only these items ran:
>
> - the host tests;
> - the real firmware in QEMU (ESP32-S3), with mosquitto and a pymodbus slave.
>
> The W5500, the RS485 hardware mode of the UART, the TCA9554, the GPIO DIs and the LED are **compile-only**. The octal PSRAM runs on the QEMU model (E2E with `E2E_PSRAM=1`, #85), not on a board. Refer to §6.

## 1. Architecture

### 1.1 Components (`components/`)

| Component | Function | Uses IDF |
|---|---|---|
| `gw_core` | **Pure C logic with no IDF. The host tests examine it:**<br>• `gw_config`: the parser and the validation of the §6 JSON.<br>• `gw_dout`: the boot sequence of the TCA9554 and the I/O polarity.<br>• `mb_rtu`: CRC and the FC01–06/15/16 frames.<br>• `mb_master`: transactions, t3.5, timeout and retries.<br>• `gw_conv`: types (up to 64 bits, `string` and register bits), scale, byte order and the Sparkplug datatype.<br>• `gw_mbap`: the Modbus TCP ↔ RTU frames of the TCP gateway function (MB-15).<br>• `gw_plan`: block reads.<br>• `gw_poll`: polling, the offline state and writes.<br>• `gw_rbe`: report by exception and deadband.<br>• `gw_debounce`, `gw_backoff`, `gw_devstate`.<br>• `gw_localio`: the DO policy without a network. It has the safe state at restart and without MQTT (IO-3) and the local DI→DO rules (IO-7). It also decides when to save the pulse counters (IO-4).<br>• `gw_netsel`: the active interface, Ethernet or Wi-Fi (NET-4). An interface is "out of service" when it cannot get to the broker (issue #57).<br>• `gw_syslog`: the conversion of log lines to RFC 5424, and a bounded ring (REL-4).<br>• `gw_tagdb`: the shared process image.<br>• `gw_cfgdoc`: the configuration document, with the secrets kept separately (masked export, import, migration). Also a reader for JSON that you paste in the console (CFG-4, CFG-6, CFG-7).<br>• `gw_auth`: SHA-256, HMAC, PBKDF2 and Basic auth for the web UI (CFG-3).<br>• `gw_button`: the BOOT button gestures (CFG-5, NET-5).<br>• `gw_buzz`: buzzer patterns, and alarms with their delay and priority (IO-6).<br>• `gw_brokersel`: the broker list, with failover and return to the primary broker (MQTT-6).<br>• `gw_phost`: the Sparkplug 3.0 primary host STATE (SPB-13).<br>• `gw_sf`: the store & forward ring buffer (SPB-14).<br>• `gw_sfspill`: the policy of the store & forward spill to the microSD card (issue #31): since issue #124, a ring of self-checking 4 KB blocks with a superblock and its boot recovery.<br>• `gw_rtc`: the PCF85063 registers ↔ UTC, with the BCD values and the OS bit (TIME-2).<br>• `gw_authlimit`: a limit on the web login attempts per address (issue #64).<br>• `gw_nvsraw`: NVS, unencrypted or encrypted, read from its raw pages (issue #67).<br>• `gw_ota`: the trial of a new image (self-check, expected mode), the pre-flight check of an image, update URLs, progress (FW-1, issue #144).<br>• `gw_bootloop`, `gw_fwlast`, `gw_cfgschema`, `gw_lkgnet`: the boot-loop counter, the record of the last update, the "since" schema of the configuration and the recovery network in a format that tolerates new fields (issue #144).<br>• `spb`: the Sparkplug codec, with nanopb.<br>• `gw_node`: the Edge Node engine, with births, deaths, seq, alias, NCMD/DCMD and rebirth. | No (only `port/gw_port_esp.c`) |
| `nanopb` | The vendored nanopb 0.4.9.1 runtime (Zlib) | No |
| `gw_rs485` | UART1 in `UART_MODE_RS485_HALF_DUPLEX` (RTS = GPIO21), available as an `mb_transport_t` | Yes |
| `gw_bus` | The task that is the **single owner of the RS485 bus**: polling and DCMD writes | Yes |
| `gw_edge` | The Sparkplug task: the esp-mqtt client (TLS, MQTT 5, broker list), bdSeq, will, backoff, primary host, store & forward, the bound on each publish, the `gw_node` engine | Yes |
| `gw_net` | W5500 on SPI (or openeth in QEMU). Optional Wi-Fi STA as an alternative or a backup (NET-4). DHCP or static IP per interface. Optional IPv6 with SLAAC and a static address (NET-7). The default route from `gw_netsel`. SNTP | Yes |
| `gw_bsp` | **The declarative description of the board** (`gw_bsp.c`): all pins, the I²C address of the expander and the active levels (DI, DO, RS485 DE, I²C, LED and color order, buzzer, W5500, microSD). The drivers read it and contain no board data. The RTC address is in `gw_core/include/gw_rtc.h` (`GW_PCF85063_ADDR`) | Yes (types) |
| `gw_board` | 8 DIs, sampled at 1 kHz, with debounce and pulse counters (IO-4, in NVS). 8 DOs on the TCA9554 (on `gw_dout`, with no pulses at boot), with the `local_io` task. This task is the **only writer of the expander** (NCMD, local rules IO-7, safe state IO-3, retries). The WS2812 status LED. The buzzer on GPIO46 (IO-6, LEDC) | Yes |
| `gw_storage` | The configuration from LittleFS, or the embedded configuration (only if the file is missing). The atomic write of a new configuration. The secrets in encrypted NVS (CFG-7). The network of the last valid configuration, for recovery mode. Factory reset and the commissioning flag (CFG-5). The microSD card of the store & forward spill (`gw_sfcard.c`, issue #31). The core dump in flash: its state, summary, read and erase (`gw_coredump.c`, issue #132) | Yes |
| `gw_web` | An HTTPS server with a certificate of the gateway itself, and a redirect from HTTP (issue #64). It has the embedded web UI (CFG-3), a JSON REST API for configuration and status (CFG-4), and Basic auth with an attempt limit. Also mDNS and `_https._tcp` (NET-6) | Yes |
| `gw_ap` | The Wi-Fi access point for commissioning (NET-5), separate from `gw_net` | Yes |
| `gw_fw` | Firmware updates (FW-1): HTTPS download (`esp_https_ota`) or upload through the web UI to the other OTA partition, the pre-flight check, verification, restart, the self-check of the new image and *rollback* (`CONFIG_BOOTLOADER_APP_ROLLBACK_ENABLE`), the boot-loop counter and safe mode, the other-slot button (issue #144) | Yes |
| `gw_log` | Remote syslog (REL-4). It installs a hook on `esp_log_set_vprintf` and copies each line to a bounded ring. A task sends the lines over UDP | Yes |
| `gw_mbtcp` | The Modbus TCP gateway (MB-15). A TCP server (lwIP) sends each MBAP request to the bus owner task and returns the response of the RTU slave. Only with `modbus_tcp.enabled` | Yes |
| `gw_shell` | The `esp_console` shell on the console: `status`, `devices`, `config [show\|load\|check]`, `restart`, `rebirth`, `mbread`, `ping`, `coredump [dump\|erase]`. `config load`, `config check`, `restart`, `rebirth`, `mbread` and `coredump erase` need `CONFIG_GW_SHELL_WRITE_COMMANDS` (default `y`). The QEMU build also has `di`, `expander`, `validation`, `storm`, `boot` and `panic`. The responses are atomic relative to the log | Yes |
| `main` | `app_main`: loads the configuration and connects the components. The modes are normal, recovery (configuration rejected) and commissioning (after a factory reset) | Yes |

Managed dependencies, **with a fixed version** (`==`) in each `idf_component.yml` and a hash in `dependencies.lock`:

- `espressif/mqtt` 1.1.0
- `espressif/w5500` 2.0.1
- `espressif/cjson` 1.7.19~2
- `espressif/led_strip` 3.1.1
- `joltwallet/littlefs` 1.23.1
- `espressif/mdns` 1.14.1

### 1.2 Tasks, queues and ownership

```
                 esp_timer (1 kHz)                 UART0 console (logs + shell)
                 gw_io: debounce DI ──┐
                                      │ flag + wake
  ┌──────────────┐  write queue       ▼            ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ rs485_bus    │◄── (8 × 64 B) ──── sparkplug ───►│ esp-mqtt (own task)      │
  │ core 1       │                    core 0        │ lwIP / esp_eth (core 0)  │
  │ prio 10      │── dirty bit ─────► prio 8        └──────────────────────────┘
  │ mb_master    │   per device       gw_node        ▲ MQTT_EVENT_DATA → pool of
  │ gw_poller    │── write done ────► (queue 24×4 B) │ 4 command buffers (1 KB)
  └──────┬───────┘                                   │
         │ writes                     reads          │
         ▼                                           │
      gw_tagdb (process image, short mutex) ◄────────┘   status_led (prio 2)
```

| Task | Core / prio | Stack | Watched by TWDT | What it owns |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `rs485_bus` (`gw_bus`) | 1 / 10 | 4 KB static | Yes. The task feeds the TWDT at each Modbus attempt | `mb_master_t`, `gw_poller_t`, UART1. The **only** task that touches the bus (MB-16) |
| `sparkplug` (`gw_edge`) | 0 / 8 | 6 KB static. After the deepest paths, at least 2 068 B stay free. The deepest paths are DCMD on a template member, DCMD with Bytes, NDATA with the DataSet, and store & forward replay of an instance and of bytes. The E2E requires ≥ 1 KB. After the births with TLS, 2 948 B stay free (`log_stacks`). The shell (`status`, `stack` line) shows the minimum of some tasks: `sparkplug`, `mqtt_task`, `rs485_bus`, `local_io`, `web_guard`, `httpd` and `httpd_redirect` | Yes. The task feeds the TWDT after **each publish** and after each operation on the microSD card (#122). It wakes at least every 1 s, and every 10 ms while the spill has card work (#124: the boot recovery, a block, a superblock, a delete) | The MQTT client and `gw_node_t` (seq, aliases, RBE state). The spill to the microSD card (`store_forward.sd`): the task does all the card operations. It does a maximum of one bounded step in each pass of its loop (§6.5). When a format needs the card (#139), the task stops the spill between two card operations, closes its files and gives the card up. Before a planned restart, the task moves the RAM ring to the card, in a time limit of 5 s (#136, §6.5). All the planned restarts go through this task. The task sends the NCMDs for the DOs to `local_io` (`gw_io_set_do`, a wait of up to 500 ms) |
| `local_io` (`gw_io`) | — / 9 | 4 KB static | Yes. The task wakes at least every 250 ms, and immediately on a DI change or an NCMD | The TCA9554, from its start with the valid configuration. The task does the NCMD writes (queue of 2) and the local DI→DO rules (IO-7; the DI sampling wakes it with a notification). It sets the safe state after `failsafe_s` with no session (IO-3, it reads `GW_ST_MQTT_UP`). It also does the retries for an expander that does not respond. It saves the pulse counters (IO-4) every `counter_save_s`. The task also owns the PCF85063 RTC on the same I²C bus (TIME-2). Before the task starts, `app_main` sets the DOs to their boot state. In recovery mode and in commissioning, a reduced form of the task runs: it only does the retries of the expander and the RTC writes (#74) |
| `esp_timer` (IDF) | — | IDF | Yes, as a TWDT user (`di_sample`). The sampling callback feeds it every 1 s | The DI sampling (1 ms callback). The budget of each MQTT publish (`pub_budget`, issue #59). The bound of a restart that another task requested (`restart_bound`, 20 s, issue #136): if the `sparkplug` task did not start the restart, this timer restarts the gateway |
| `status_led` | — / 2 | 2.5 KB | No (not critical) | The LED RMT |
| `console` (`gw_shell`) | — / 2 | 4 KB | No (not critical) | The console UART (`esp_console` REPL). The task reads `gw_tagdb` and `gw_status`. The writes and the Modbus read go through `gw_storage`, `gw_edge` and the bus queue |
| `syslog` (`gw_log`, only with `syslog.host`) | — / 2 | 3.5 KB static | No (not critical) | The UDP socket and the consumer side of the ring |
| `modbus_tcp` (`gw_mbtcp`, only with `modbus_tcp.enabled`) | 0 / 3 | 4 KB static | No (not critical). All waits have a bound: `select` of 1 s, send of 2 s, bus response of 8 s | The listen socket, up to 2 clients (`CONFIG_GW_MBTCP_MAX_CLIENTS`) and the single transaction in progress, which `rs485_bus` executes |
| `httpd` (`gw_web`) | — / 3 | 10 KB (HTTPS) or 8 KB (HTTP) | No (not critical). A *watchdog* of its own cuts its sessions if a request holds the task for more than `CONFIG_GW_WEB_STALL_S` (issue #110) | The HTTPS server. The task validates and saves configurations (buffers in PSRAM if present). With HTTPS, a second `httpd` of 3 KB only redirects from HTTP. The two tasks have the name `httpd`, because esp_http_server does not let you select the name. The shell shows them by their *handle*, as `httpd` and `httpd_redirect` in the `stack` line (issue #110). Measured in QEMU: `httpd` 5 408–6 252 B free, `httpd_redirect` 1 096–1 724 B |
| `fw_update` (`gw_fw`, only during a download or a boot of the other slot) | 0 / 2 | 8 KB (from the heap, released at the end) | No (not critical). Each network wait has a bound of 10 s. The task yields the CPU at each block, and the TWDT watches the IDLE task of its core | The HTTPS download and the write of the passive OTA partition, or the verification of the other slot for the button of #144. A web upload writes from `httpd`. The self-check of a new image runs in the `main` task, which stays until the end of the trial (issue #144: the `fw_trial` task of #108 is removed) |
| `fw_park`, `fw_install` (`gw_fw`, QEMU build only, `CONFIG_GW_FW_PARK_OTHER_CORE`) | 1 / 23 and 0 / 2 | 2 KB and 6 KB from the heap | No | Only while the app maps the flash for a verification: `fw_park` keeps core 1 in a wait loop. `fw_install` runs that step on core 0 when the caller is on core 1 (§6.10, issues #107 and #111) |
| `card_format` (`gw_storage`, only during a format of the microSD card, issues #139 and #137) | 0 / 1 | 5 KB from the internal heap, released at the end | No (not critical). The task waits on each SDMMC transfer, so the IDLE task of core 0 runs, and the TWDT watches it | The microSD card during a format (§6.5). The shell and the web API only start the task. The firmware creates it for each format and deletes it after the format: a card gets a format one time in its life, so the task keeps no RAM in normal operation. Stack: the deepest path is `f_mkfs` → `disk_write` → `sdmmc_write_sectors` → the host driver, approximately 1.2 KB by `-fstack-usage`, plus a log line. In QEMU, the simulated format (`card format simulate`) kept 3 916 B free, and 3 108 B when it first stopped the spill (PSRAM variant). The real format adds the `f_mkfs` path, so approximately 2 KB stay free by this estimate. The task writes its free stack in the log at its end (#131 measures the real format) |
| `boot_button` (`gw_board`) | — / 2 | 3 KB static | No (not critical) | GPIO0 (sampled every 20 ms). The task requests the confirmation beeps from the buzzer |
| `buzzer` (`gw_board`) | — / 2 | 2.5 KB static | No (not critical) | GPIO46 and LEDC channel 7 (IO-6). The task reads the `gw_status` flags, selects the alarm (`gw_buzz`) and starts or stops the tone. It runs every 20 ms while the tone sounds, and every 250 ms in silence (a beep request wakes it) |
| `mdns` (component) | 0 / 1 | 4 KB | No | The mDNS responder |
| `web_guard` (`gw_web`, issue #110) | — / 1 | 2.5 KB static | No (not critical) | The *watchdog* of the web servers. Every 1 s, it sends a *ping* to each server. If a server is held, it calls `shutdown()` on the sessions of that server |
| esp-mqtt (`mqtt_task`), lwIP (`tcpip`), Ethernet RX (`w5500_tsk` / `emac_rx`), Wi-Fi (only with `network.wifi`), event loop (`sys_evt`) | **0** / IDF | IDF | IDLE watched | The event loop owns `gw_netsel` (the link and IP events, and the *failback* timer) |

**Affinity.** All the network stack runs on core 0, away from the RS485 bus on core 1. These settings do this:

- `CONFIG_LWIP_TCPIP_TASK_AFFINITY_CPU0`;
- `CONFIG_MQTT_USE_CORE_0`;
- `ETH_MAC_FLAG_PIN_TO_CORE` in `gw_net` (`app_main` installs the driver, on core 0);
- the default event loop and the Wi-Fi task, which IDF already pins to core 0.

The `status` shell command shows the core of each task (`tasks rs485_bus 1 sparkplug 0 tcpip 0 …`). The E2E checks it.

**Concurrency.** The producers never block the consumer:

- The device updates and the DI updates are **atomic flags**, plus an optional "wake" event. If a wake is lost, no state is lost: the Sparkplug task reconciles at each loop.
- Data that must not be lost goes through bounded static queues:
  - commands (a pool of 4 buffers);
  - write confirmations;
  - DCMD writes to the bus (a queue of 8).
- The only shared memory is `gw_tagdb`. A mutex protects it, and the code copies it only inside the critical section.
- **cJSON operates under a lock of its own (issue #106).** Its *hooks* (the allocator), its error pointer and the out-of-memory flag of `gw_config` are global to the process. Several tasks parse JSON: the web UI (`PUT /api/config`, `POST /api/firmware`), the shell (`config load`) and the boot. `gw_config_parse`, `gw_cfgdoc_apply`, `gw_cfgdoc_export` and `gw_ota_parse_request` hold `gw_port_json_lock()` during the full operation. This lock is recursive: a static FreeRTOS *mutex*, or a pthread mutex on the host. Before, a URL upload through the web UI during a shell `config load` could cause a false "out of memory" error, or hide a real one.

  A host test with two threads shows this: without the lock, the test fails in each run. This lock is not the `gw_tagdb` lock. A validation holds it for tens of ms up to some seconds. The polling and the publish do not use it.

**MQTT and Sparkplug connection:**

- The auto-reconnect function of esp-mqtt is disabled.
- Before **each** attempt, the task takes the next bdSeq (SPB-3, issue #161). The bdSeq is in RAM only: it starts at 0 at each boot and increases by exactly 1 for each MQTT CONNECT packet (`tck-id-topics-nbirth-bdseq-increment`). The task sets the matching NDEATH as the *will* (`esp_mqtt_set_config`). Then it connects again after an exponential backoff with jitter (1–60 s).
  - esp-mqtt sends the CONNECT packet only after the transport (TCP, and TLS) connects. If the transport does not connect, the `MQTT_EVENT_ERROR` of the attempt comes while the transport has no socket. Then the attempt sent no CONNECT packet, and the next attempt takes the same bdSeq again. The same applies when the client cannot start (no memory).
  - An attempt that can have sent its CONNECT packet keeps its bdSeq: a CONNECT write that failed, no CONNACK, a refused CONNACK. The reason: the broker can publish the *will* of that attempt later, and its bdSeq must never be the bdSeq of a newer NBIRTH (`gw_bdseq_t` in `gw_node`).
- esp-mqtt can report the same lost connection two times (a failed write, then the read error). If a retry is already scheduled, the second report does not count as a new attempt.
- The backoff goes back to the minimum **only when the session stayed stable** for one full keepalive (60 s maximum) after the NBIRTH. A CONNACK alone does not reset the backoff. Thus, the attempts increase to the maximum with a broker that accepts the connection and cuts it immediately (ACL, duplicate client_id, connection limit). A failure of `esp_mqtt_client_init` (no memory) waits for the same backoff.
- On connection, the task sends a SUBSCRIBE (NCMD and DCMD/+) and **waits for its SUBACK** (up to 5 s) before the NBIRTH and the DBIRTHs. Thus a node that the broker shows as online always receives its commands. The task closes the connection with DISCONNECT and tries again with backoff in these cases:
  - the task cannot send the SUBSCRIBE;
  - the broker rejects a topic (code 0x80);
  - the SUBACK does not arrive.

  The broker then discards the *will*, because there was no NBIRTH.
- If the births went out without a valid time (no SNTP and no RTC time, §6.4), the task does a *rebirth* when SNTP synchronizes. Then the timestamps are valid. Births with the RTC time get no *rebirth*.
- **Interface change (NET-4).** With Ethernet and Wi-Fi configured, the task binds the MQTT socket to the active interface (`SO_BINDTODEVICE`). When `gw_netsel` selects a different interface, the session cannot move. The task then does these steps:
  - It publishes the NDEATH in an orderly sequence. If the old interface does not operate, the NDEATH is lost, and the broker publishes the *will* later.
  - It destroys the client.
  - It connects a new client through the new interface, with a new bdSeq, an NBIRTH and all the DBIRTHs.

  With only Ethernet, the task does not bind the socket and the interface does not change: all operates as in the MVP.

**Interface selection (`gw_netsel`, NET-4):**

- An interface is *usable* when it has a link (cable or association) and an IP address.
- Each interface has a rank. With `role: backup`, Ethernet is the preferred interface. With `primary`, Wi-Fi is the preferred interface.
- When no interface is active, the first usable interface becomes active immediately, independently of its rank.
- If the active interface is no longer usable, the best usable interface replaces it immediately (*failover*).
- The preferred interface gets the session back only after it was usable for `failback_s` continuous seconds. This is *failback* with hysteresis: a cable that connects and disconnects many times does not cut the session at each bounce.
- **Interface without a path to the broker (issue #57).** All brokers can fail on the active interface, while that interface has a link and an IP address. Then the session moves to the other interface. The gateway does not select the failed interface again for 60 s (`CONFIG_GW_NET_NO_SERVICE_MIN_S`). Each new failure in sequence makes this time two times longer, up to 30 min (`CONFIG_GW_NET_NO_SERVICE_MAX_S`). A stable session sets it back to the minimum (§6.6).
- The default route follows the active interface, and DNS and SNTP follow the default route (`CONFIG_ESP_NETIF_SET_DNS_PER_DEFAULT_NETIF`). The `esp_netif` route priorities have the same order (Ethernet 50; Wi-Fi 20 as backup or 100 as primary). By default, STA has 100 and would win.

### 1.3 Memory (REL-2)

- **Static limits:** 16 devices, 64 tags per device, 256 tags in total.
- **The firmware assumes the PSRAM of the board (issue #135).** The board has 8 MB of octal PSRAM (ESP32-S3-WROOM-1U-N16R8). The firmware does not support a board without PSRAM. A board whose PSRAM the boot does not detect runs only the fault mode (refer to the item below).
- **All own memory has a fixed size.** It is static, or the firmware allocates it at boot or for one operation:
  - **In PSRAM** (`gw_storage_malloc`, `gw_storage_calloc` and `gw_storage_big_alloc`). When the PSRAM is full, the allocation fails: these functions do not use internal RAM in its place. Thus the internal RAM stays for Wi-Fi, lwIP, esp-mqtt, the task stacks and DMA. This applies to the `gw_config_t` configuration (32 304 B), the `gw_node_t` engine (21 480 B) and its payload buffer of 16 KB (`GW_SPB_PAYLOAD_MAX`). It also applies to the TLS sessions (§6.5.1), the buffers of a configuration validation, the FW-1 transfers, the syslog ring and the buffers of the shell and the web server.
  - Store & forward (SPB-14, only with `sparkplug.store_forward`): 40 000 samples of 24 B (960 KB) in PSRAM (`CONFIG_GW_SF_SAMPLES`).
  - The spill to the microSD card (only with `store_forward.sd`, issue #124): one allocation of 10 KB in PSRAM. It holds two blocks (one to write, one to read) and the delete queue. The SDMMC build also allocates a DMA buffer of 4 KB in internal RAM. The SDMMC driver uses it for each transfer from and to PSRAM. Without it, the driver allocated up to 8 KB of internal RAM for each transfer, in the `sparkplug` task (#122).
  - The format of the microSD card (issue #139, only on request, only with PSRAM): the `card_format` stack (5 KB), a buffer of 16 KB (4 KB when no block of 16 KB is free), the card descriptor and a FatFs volume, in internal DMA-capable RAM. The SDMMC writes then need no bounce buffer. The task releases all of it at the end. The state of the card and of the format is static: 268 B of internal `.bss` and `.data` in the QEMU build (276 B in the SDMMC build).
  - The internal `.bss`:
    - `gw_edge`, with the 6 KB stack and 4 command buffers of 1.2 KB;
    - `gw_bus`, approximately 15 KB (planner, polling and a 4 KB stack);
    - `gw_core`, approximately 15 KB (`gw_tagdb`, 56 B per tag since MB-6);
    - `gw_mbtcp`, approximately 6 KB (a 4 KB stack and buffers, also when it is off).
- **A board without PSRAM: the fault mode (issue #135).** When the boot does not detect the PSRAM (a defective or missing chip), it continues (`CONFIG_SPIRAM_IGNORE_NOTFOUND`), only to report the fault. The gateway does not start: the firmware does not load the configuration, and there is no polling, no MQTT and no Modbus TCP. All DOs go off.
  - The log gives `main: PSRAM not detected: the board has 8 MB of PSRAM and the boot did not find it (a defective or missing chip); the gateway does not start (issue #135)` at boot, and again every 10 s.
  - The shell `status` gives the line `FAULT    PSRAM not detected: …`. `config` and `mbread` give the same cause.
  - `GET /api/status` gives `"mode": "fault"` and the cause in `"fault"`. The web UI shows the cause in the red banner. `POST /api/rebirth` and `POST /api/modbus/read` respond `409` with the cause.
  - The LED shows the state of a configuration error.
  - The network, the HTTPS server, mDNS, the shell and the access point (NET-5) start as in recovery mode, from internal RAM. The network uses the settings of the last valid configuration, or DHCP on Ethernet. The web password is the stored one, or the commissioning password.
  - A new image on trial (FW-1) goes back to the previous image at once (`no memory to start (no PSRAM)`, #144), because it cannot start the gateway. A firmware upload through the web UI stays possible.

  The E2E examines the fault mode in the PSRAM variant (scenario `nopsram`). It boots the PSRAM build on QEMU without the octal PSRAM model.
- **The QEMU build without PSRAM (`CONFIG_GW_EMU_NO_PSRAM`).** Only the QEMU build of `sdkconfig.qemu` (`CONFIG_GW_IO_SIMULATED` without `CONFIG_SPIRAM`) runs the gateway without PSRAM. It sets the hidden option `CONFIG_GW_EMU_NO_PSRAM`. In this build, the same functions use internal RAM, the payload buffer is 6 KB (`CONFIG_GW_SPB_PAYLOAD_INTERNAL`) and the ring has 512 samples (12 KB). The spill does not start. A birth that does not fit the 6 KB buffer is logged and not published. Such a message uses no seq number (`tck-id-payloads-sequence-num-incrementing`, #158), and a DBIRTH that does not fit leaves its device unborn (§6.5, #76). The E2E uses this build only for the scenarios that do not depend on memory, because they are faster without PSRAM (§4.3). A build without `CONFIG_SPIRAM` and without `CONFIG_GW_IO_SIMULATED` does not compile, and `GW_SDKCONFIG_REQUIRED` has `CONFIG_SPIRAM` (§3.3).
- **Why not `EXT_RAM_BSS_ATTR`:** To put `.bss` in PSRAM, IDF requires `CONFIG_SPIRAM_ALLOW_BSS_SEG_EXTERNAL_MEMORY`. In IDF, this option is not compatible with `CONFIG_SPIRAM_IGNORE_NOTFOUND`. Thus a board with a PSRAM that IDF does not detect would stop in the boot, and it could not report the fault. With the allocation at boot, the fault mode can run from internal RAM.
- **Margin of the internal RAM (#80).** The internal RAM is shared with Wi-Fi, lwIP, esp-mqtt, the task stacks and DMA, so the E2E still measures it. Since issue #135, the check runs in the PSRAM variant, the configuration of the board (the `tls` scenario, node `GW29`). The thresholds did not change: ≥ 8 KB minimum free and ≥ 17 KB largest block at the validation. §4.3 gives the values of each run. Before #135, the reference was the QEMU build without PSRAM, where the margin was small. The values below are from that build, in the worst case. The worst case is MQTT over TLS 1.3, full births, one open HTTPS session, and a `PUT /api/config?dry_run=1` of the full document on a second session. The results of two runs are:
  - **minimum free internal RAM: 14 304 to 14 372 B** since boot;
  - **largest free block at the validation peak: 30 720 to 31 744 B.** The validation builds a second `gw_config_t` of 32 KB contiguous.

  The E2E requires ≥ 8 KB and ≥ 17 KB. This is the memory that must be available at that time: an MQTT command with its TLS record, the lwIP pbufs and one full TLS record of 16 KB. The shell (`status`, `heap` and `stack` lines) shows these values on any gateway. The items below (dynamic buffers, FW-1, #91) were measured in the build without PSRAM too.
- **mbedTLS with dynamic buffers** (`CONFIG_MBEDTLS_DYNAMIC_BUFFER`, #80): each TLS session reserves its record buffers only while a record is in progress. With fixed buffers (16 KB of input per session), MQTT over TLS and two HTTPS sessions fragmented the internal RAM. No contiguous block of 32 KB stayed: the `PUT` responded 503, but 92 KB were free.
- **FW-1, with no update in progress, does not use memory on the validation path (#80).** With the OTA code added, the `PUT` of #80 responded 503 (`memoria insuficiente para procesar la configuración`). The print of the document requested a block of 10 643 B, and the largest remaining block was 8 704 B. The QEMU *heap* (the IDF TLSF) rounds each allocation to its size class, so a few hundred bytes are important. With only `main`, the same case is one or two KB from the limit. In one run, a diagnostic array of 1.9 KB in `.bss` was sufficient to make `main` a0b4a10 also respond 503.

  FW-1 does these steps so that it adds no memory:
  - It creates the backstop timer and the restart timer only when necessary (an image on trial).
  - It reserves the download URL, the server CA and the header of an uploaded image at the start of a transfer (in PSRAM). It releases them at the end.
  - It formats the state in place, under a *mutex*, with no copy buffers.
  - It puts the plain state data (≈ 590 B) in the RTC FAST memory. This memory has 8 KB, both cores can access it, and the *heap* almost does not use it.
  - `Firmware Update/State` has up to 95 characters. Thus the Sparkplug engine stays in the 21 504 B class, and does not go to the 22 528 B class. It was 21 480 B; with the device state bits of #173 it is 21 488 B (measured with `xtensa-esp32s3-elf-nm`).

  Result: the DRAM `.bss` is 160 B more than in `main`. In two runs of `--only tls`, the `PUT` responded 200. The largest block at the validation was 26 624 and 28 672 B, and the minimum free RAM was 13 632 and 13 644 B (thresholds 17 KB and 8 KB). The #84 check also passed.
- **The print of the validated document requests only one block of its size (#91).** Before, `cJSON_Print` made its buffer two times larger at each step, and copied both buffers at each move. The change from tabs to spaces requested one more buffer of two times the text size. With the #80 document (`GW29`, 12 762 B indented, 10 687 B with tabs), the result was a block of **21 376 B**. The print held up to **32 KB**, in addition to the second `gw_config_t`. The block of 10 643 B that caused the 503 was an intermediate step.

  Now `gw_cfgdoc` writes the same text in two passes (count, reserve the exact size, write): **one block of 12 763 B**, and nothing more. In QEMU, without PSRAM, the largest free block immediately before the request was 31 744 B. Thus **18 981 B stay as margin** (the E2E requires ≥ 4 KB, the #91 check). In the #80 case, the minimum free internal RAM since boot increased from 13 572 B to **37 180 B**. The log of each validation shows these values (`validation: document N B, largest request N B, largest internal block before it N B`). The text is the same as before, byte for byte (a host test compares it with `cJSON_Print`).
- The polling, encoding and publish path does not allocate memory. The spill to the microSD card allocates only when it opens a file (issues #122 and #124): the FatFs file buffer (512 B). The files stay open between the operations, and the 8.3 names need no name buffer (`CONFIG_FATFS_LFN_NONE`). The gateway opens an extent of the ring one time for its writes and one time for its reads, not for each block. The two superblock files stay open.
- The firmware allocates the long-lived structures one time at boot: the configuration, the engine and its payload buffer. At boot, it also allocates the LittleFS read and the cJSON tree, and releases them before the tasks start (§3.4).
- The firmware allocates some buffers only during one operation, and releases them at the end (in PSRAM):
  - a configuration validation: the second `gw_config_t`, the request body and the cJSON tree;
  - a firmware transfer: the buffers of FW-1 and the `fw_update` stack;
  - some shell commands: for example, the document of `config load` and the firmware state that `status` shows.
- lwIP and esp-mqtt allocate memory internally (refer to §6).

## 2. Board data marked "verify"

**Sources:**

- The official demo `ESP32-S3-POE-ETH-8DI-8DO-Demo.zip` (Arduino, `files.waveshare.com/wiki/ESP32-S3-POE-ETH-8DI-8DO/`).
- The Waveshare wiki.
- The ESPHome definition of this same board, `Waveshare-ESP32-S3-ETH-8DI-8DO` (`esphome-devices`, `config.yaml`).
- The ESPHome definition of the sister board `Waveshare-ESP32-S3-ETH-8DI-8RO`. It has the same GPIOs for DI, I²C, RTC, LED and buzzer. Its relays are **not** inverted, thus it is not evidence for the DO polarity.

The search found no published schematic.

| Data | Finding | Evidence | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| DO expander | **TCA9554 at 0x20** | Demo: `TCA9554_ADDRESS 0x20` (`WS_TCA9554PWR.h`). ESPHome of the sister board: `pca9554 address: 0x20` | High |
| DO polarity | **Low level on the expander pin = DO active** (Darlington output) | **For:** the demo boots with `Dout_Init()` → `TCA9554PWR_Init(0x00, 0xFF)`, all high (a demo does not energize 8 loads at power-on). Its DI→DO mode (`WS_DIN.cpp`, `DIN_Inverse_Enable = 0`) copies the raw level (DI at rest = 1 → DO = 1 = off). The ESPHome definition of this board (8DI-8DO) declares `inverted: true` on the 8 DOs. The Zephyr implementation got the same result (`GPIO_ACTIVE_LOW`). **Against:** the demo calls `Dout_Open()` at the HIGH level, and `"CH1":1` over MQTT sets the pin high. Also, the wiki (a copy of the relay wiki) says "ALL_ON … high level (on state)" | **Medium.** Configurable: `CONFIG_GW_DO_ACTIVE_LOW` (default `y`, the most probable hypothesis). **Measure with a multimeter before you connect loads** |
| RTC | **PCF85063 at 0x51** (SDA 42, SCL 41, INT 40) | Demo: `PCF85063_ADDRESS (0x51)` (`WS_PCF85063.h`); ESPHome: `platform: pcf85063` | High (TIME-2: the firmware uses the RTC; the real I²C has no test) |
| DI polarity | GPIO with pull-up; **active input = level 0** (inverted) | Demo: `pinMode(DIN_PIN_CHx, INPUT_PULLUP)` and the rest state `0xFF`; ESPHome: `INPUT_PULLUP` + `inverted: true` | Medium-high. Configurable: `CONFIG_GW_DI_ACTIVE_LOW` |
| Buzzer | **Passive** (needs PWM) | Demo: `ledcAttach(46, 1000 Hz, 8 bits)` with duty 200/255; ESPHome uses `rtttl` on `ledc` | Medium. The firmware drives it with LEDC at 2.7 kHz (`CONFIG_GW_BUZZER_FREQ_HZ`). An active buzzer would need level control (`gw_bsp.buzzer.passive = false`) |
| LED | WS2812 on GPIO38, **RGB byte order** (not GRB) | Demo: `neopixelWrite(pin, green, red, blue)` (it swaps R and G); ESPHome: `rgb_order: RGB` | Medium |
| RS485 | UART1, TX 17, RX 18, RTS 21, `UART_MODE_RS485_HALF_DUPLEX` mode | Demo `WS_RS485.cpp`: `setPins(-1,-1,-1,21)` + `setMode(UART_MODE_RS485_HALF_DUPLEX)` | High |
| W5500 | SPI 13/14/15, CS 16, INT 12, RST 39 | The wiki and `WS_ETH.h` | High |
| Console | USB-C → CH34x USB-UART bridge on UART0 (GPIO 43/44) | The wiki has a link to the CH34XSER driver | Medium. If there are no logs, try `CONFIG_ESP_CONSOLE_USB_SERIAL_JTAG` (the shell also operates there) |

All these data are in `components/gw_bsp/gw_bsp.c`, except the RTC address, which is in `components/gw_core/include/gw_rtc.h` (`GW_PCF85063_ADDR`). Two polarities (DI and DO) come only from indirect evidence. You can invert them in `menuconfig` (menu `Gateway: board`). The other data are fixed. DO boot (`gw_dout`): the latch gets the OFF level of each channel (0xFF with active low) **before** the pins change to outputs. The firmware inverts the write and the read-back according to the polarity mask.

***Strapping* pins** with board peripherals: GPIO0 (BOOT), GPIO3 (CAN RX, JTAG), GPIO45 (SD D0) and GPIO46 (buzzer). The firmware configures GPIO0 and GPIO46 only from `app_main`, never before. The firmware does not use GPIO3. GPIO45 is the D0 line of the microSD card: the firmware uses it only when `sparkplug.store_forward.sd` is on (refer to the warning in §6.5).

## 3. Build, flash and configure

### 3.1 ESP-IDF

```bash
mkdir -p ~/sdk/idf && cd ~/sdk/idf
git clone --depth 1 --branch v6.1 --recursive --shallow-submodules https://github.com/espressif/esp-idf.git
cd esp-idf && IDF_TOOLS_PATH=~/sdk/idf/tools ./install.sh esp32s3
export IDF_TOOLS_PATH=~/sdk/idf/tools && . ~/sdk/idf/esp-idf/export.sh
```

**Supported IDF versions: 6.x** (tested with 6.1). `gw_edge` stops the build outside this range (`#error`). The reason: it chains the TLS verification onto the callback that `esp_crt_bundle_attach()` installs (issue #77). Before you extend the range, make sure that this function still installs its verification with `mbedtls_ssl_conf_verify()`.

### 3.2 Firmware

```bash
cd idf
idf.py set-target esp32s3 build      # QA-2: 0 warnings
idf.py size
idf.py -p /dev/ttyUSB0 flash monitor # flashes the bootloader, the table, the app (ota_0) and the LittleFS partition
```

Result of `idf.py size` (clean build with `idf.py set-target esp32s3 build`, 1219/1219 targets, 0 warnings, `sdkconfig` regenerated from `sdkconfig.defaults`; Wi-Fi compiled, `CONFIG_GW_WIFI=y`):

| Region | Used |
|---|---|
| Flash code (`.text`) | 1 053 676 B |
| Flash data (`.rodata`, etc.) | 296 412 B |
| DIRAM | 194 720 B of 341 760 (**57.0 %**): `.bss` 104 368, `.text` 65 975, `.data` 24 377 |
| RTC FAST | 616 B of 8 192 (almost all of it is the FW-1 state: #80) |
| IRAM | 16 384 B (100 %, this is normal on the S3: the remaining IRAM code goes in DIRAM) |
| Total image | **1 457 452 B** (35 % of a 4 MB OTA partition) |

**With issue #145 (branch `fix/spill-canonical-hash`, on `main` 297c081, measured against `main` with the same command):**

- flash code 1 069 696 B (+224: the configuration hash from the list of fields);
- flash data 308 620 B (+0);
- DIRAM 198 448 B (+0). The hash runs one time at boot, with no allocation;
- image of 1 485 884 B (+224).

The production build has 0 warnings. Unsigned, it is 1 517 508 B (+232); signed, the app is 1 576 960 B (37.6 % of an OTA slot, the same as before).

**With issue #136 (branch `fix/flush-before-restart`, on `main` 48311a7, measured against `main` with the same command):**

- flash code 1 069 472 B (+1 212: the move of the RAM ring before a planned restart, the bound of a requested restart);
- flash data 308 620 B (+1 232, the log lines);
- DIRAM 198 448 B (+0: the new static variables fit in the alignment padding of `.bss`). The bound of a requested restart is one `esp_timer`, which the firmware allocates at boot;
- image of 1 485 660 B (+2 444).

The production build has 0 warnings. Unsigned, it is 1 517 276 B; signed, the app is 1 576 960 B (37.6 % of an OTA slot, the same as before).

**With issue #139 (branch `feature/card-format-web`, on `main` 24462f4, measured against `main` with the same command):**

- flash code 1 068 260 B (+2 552: the format task, the mount with the reason, the card and format states, the web API);
- flash data 307 388 B (+5 024: the page has the microSD section, +2 827 B, now 19 948 B);
- DIRAM 198 448 B (+288: `.bss` +160 and `.data` +128, the state of the card and of the format and the owner of the card);
- image of 1 483 216 B (+7 704).

**With issue #124 (branch `feature/sd-ring-step2`, on `main` 565dbfe, measured against `main` with the same command):**

- flash code 1 065 708 B (+6 720: the ring, its recovery, the CRC-32C, the format command);
- flash data 302 364 B (+2 448);
- DIRAM 198 160 B (+112: `.bss` +96, the status of the ring for the shell; `.data` +16). The state of the spill (704 B) is not in `.bss` any more. The firmware allocates it at boot, in PSRAM, only when the spill starts;
- image of 1 475 512 B (+9 184).

The production build has 0 warnings. Unsigned, it is 1 507 192 B; signed, the app is 1 576 960 B (37.6 % of an OTA slot).

**With issue #122 (branch `fix/sd-spill-step1`, on `main` 3e6c982, measured against `main` with the same command):**

- flash code 1 058 988 B (+364);
- flash data 299 916 B (+240);
- DIRAM 198 048 B (+304: `.bss` +256 for the times of the card operations and the state of the deletes, `.data` +48 for the table of open files);
- image of 1 466 328 B (+652).

The production build has 0 warnings (1200/1200 targets). Unsigned, it is 1 497 996 B; signed, the app is 1 511 424 B.

**With issues #91, #100 and #103 (branch `fix/web-hardening`):**

- flash code 1 053 944 B (+268);
- flash data 297 052 B (+640: the document printer and the CSRF check);
- DIRAM 194 736 B (+16 of `.bss`: the CSRF header buffers are on the stack of the web server);
- `rs485_spb_gateway.bin` of 1 458 480 B.

The production build still has 0 warnings. The unsigned app has the same size (1 507 328 B; signed, 1 511 424 B). The measurements below are from before these issues.

Measured on `feature/firmware`, with `main` a0b4a10 included, the fixes for issues #93, #94, #98 and #99 (item 5), and the second and third review of #94. The image is 1 148 B larger than before these fixes (1 456 304 B). The cause is the NVS migration that is safe from power cuts, and its copy. DIRAM is the same.

The production build (`sdkconfig.production`, with Secure Boot and flash encryption, §3.7) compiles with 0 warnings (1222/1222 targets). Unsigned, it is 1 485 940 B (before: 1 476 536). Signed, the bootloader is 40 960 B and the app is 1 511 424 B. Measured before on `feature/diag-ram` (on `main` 6bec093): image 1 415 984 B, production 1 425 024 B.

The dynamic buffers of mbedTLS (#80) add 3 304 B to the image and no static DIRAM. The `Node Info/Modbus TCP` table (#81) is in the Sparkplug engine, which the firmware allocates at boot.

**Cost of FW-1 to FW-3 and of issues #67, #76, #77 and #78** (on `main` 0ca43df, 1 396 040 B):

- image +38 868 B (`esp_https_ota` and the HTTP client, image verification, `gw_fw`, the NVS migration, the web card);
- DIRAM +1 432 B (`.bss` +1 264: the state of the update, of the trial and of the upload).

The download task (8 KB) and the TLS session (approximately 40 KB) exist only during a download. The bootloader with *rollback* becomes a few hundred bytes larger.

**Cost of the local I/O (IO-3, IO-4, IO-6, IO-7)**, measured on its branch: `.bss` +7 440 B (90 128 → 97 568). This memory holds the static stacks of the `local_io` task (4 KB) and of the buzzer (2.5 KB), the NCMD queues, the counters and the rule state. Image +9 444 B. The Sparkplug engine (in PSRAM if present) becomes 64 B larger (published counters).

**Cost of the Modbus work (MB-6, MB-11, MB-12, MB-14, MB-15, SPB-15):** measured on `main` with config-web, `.bss` +22 544 B (67 584 → 90 128) and image +11 596 B. Measured before on the MVP: `.bss` +19 824 B (57 792 → 77 616). Approximately 13 KB of this memory is:

- the tag value of 56 B (before: 24 B) in the process image (`gw_core` +8.9 KB);
- the working copy of the polling and the write queue (`gw_bus` +4.6 KB, also the scheduler by class);
- the shell copy (+2 KB).

5.8 KB is the Modbus TCP server (`gw_mbtcp`). The firmware reserves this memory also when the server is off. Flash +13 KB.

The firmware allocates the configuration and the Sparkplug engine (approximately 53 KB) at boot, in PSRAM if present (§1.3). Thus they are not in `.bss`.

**Cost of the metrics and the time (SPB-16 to SPB-19, TIME-2, TIME-3, #74, #75):** +15 724 B of image (codec for properties, DataSet, Bytes and templates; RTC; diagnostics) and +48 B of DIRAM (`.bss`). The payload buffer moved out of `gw_node_t`, and the stack of the Sparkplug task stays at 6 KB. At run time, with PSRAM:

- the configuration becomes 2.7 KB larger (engineering range as two Float per tag, device type);
- the engine becomes approximately 2.5 KB larger (metrics with properties, templates, a snapshot of the counters);
- the payload buffer becomes 16 KB.

Without PSRAM, the buffer is 6 KB (`CONFIG_GW_SPB_PAYLOAD_INTERNAL`). The production build (`sdkconfig.production`) compiles with 0 warnings.

**Cost of TLS, MQTT 5, the broker list, the primary host, store & forward and HTTPS (MQTT-4 to MQTT-8, SPB-13, SPB-14, issues #57/#58/#59/#64):**

- +90 096 B of image (1 210 192 → 1 300 288): mbedTLS with TLS 1.3 and the X.509 writer, the common subset of the CA bundle, `esp_https_server`, the MQTT 5 client;
- +4 064 B of DIRAM (`.bss`): the certificate and the key of the web server (2 KB), and the state of the connection and of the access limit.

At run time:

- A TLS session (MQTT or HTTPS) uses approximately 40 KB of mbedTLS, in PSRAM if present.
- The store & forward ring uses 960 KB of PSRAM (or 12 KB of internal RAM), only with `sparkplug.store_forward`.
- The Sparkplug engine becomes approximately 5 KB larger. This is the history batch (128 records of 24 B) and the texts of one message. Since issue #31, the batch has 64-bit samples and texts.

The dump to microSD (issue #31) reserves no memory when it is off. When it is on, it uses the memory that FAT and the SDMMC driver take when they mount the card (not measured: there is no board). The records go from the ring to the card without an intermediate copy.

**Cost of the web configuration, the shell, commissioning and secrets (CFG-3 to CFG-7, NET-5, NET-6):** +113 116 B of image and +10 408 B of DIRAM. Of this, `.bss` is +9 792: the static 3 KB stack of the button, the 1 KB buffer for shell responses, the 1 KB status buffer and the tag values of the web, mutex and semaphores. At run time, the firmware allocates these items from the heap:

- the HTTP server (8 KB stack and sockets);
- mDNS (4 KB stack);
- the buffers of a configuration in validation (the document, the `gw_config_t` of 32 304 B and the cJSON tree). The firmware allocates these buffers only during a save, and in PSRAM if present.

**Cost of Wi-Fi (NET-4).** Before: DIRAM 153 121 B (44.8 %) and an image of 735 029 B. The Wi-Fi driver adds **27 327 B of static DIRAM** (measured in its commit) (`.bss` +11 504, `.text` +8 352, `.data` +7 471). It adds this memory also when the configuration does not enable Wi-Fi. It also adds 347 KB of flash. The project already moved out of IRAM all that IDF permits (`CONFIG_ESP_WIFI_IRAM_OPT=n`, `CONFIG_ESP_WIFI_RX_IRAM_OPT=n`). When Wi-Fi starts, its dynamic buffers and those of lwIP go to PSRAM when possible (`CONFIG_SPIRAM_TRY_ALLOCATE_WIFI_LWIP`). Nobody measured the internal heap that Wi-Fi uses at run time (there is no board).

If you do not need Wi-Fi, set `CONFIG_GW_WIFI=n` to recover all of this memory.

**Cost of the remote syslog (REL-4):** 4.6 KB of `.bss` (the static 3.5 KB stack of the sender task, and the buffers for one line and one datagram). The firmware allocates the ring (32 lines of approximately 240 B) at boot, only if `syslog.host` is set, and in PSRAM if present.

Partition table (`partitions.csv`, 16 MB): `nvs` 24 KB, `otadata`, `phy_init`, `ota_0`/`ota_1` 4 MB each (FW-1, §3.6), `storage` LittleFS 1 MB and `coredump` 64 KB.

**Core dump (REL-1, issue #132).** A *panic* (or the TWDT) saves an ELF dump in the `coredump` partition. Since IDF 6.0, ELF is the only format; the option `CONFIG_ESP_COREDUMP_DATA_FORMAT_ELF` no longer exists. The dump stays in flash until you erase it, also after more restarts. With a USB cable, `idf.py coredump-info` (or `coredump-debug`) reads it. Without a cable (for example, a gateway in a panel), the running firmware gives it:

- **Sparkplug:** the NBIRTH metric `Node Info/Core Dump` (Boolean, alias 57) is `true` while a dump is in flash. After an erase, an NDATA with alias 57 only sends `false`. Thus the SCADA can tell the operator that a crash occurred.
- **REST API:** `GET /api/coredump` downloads the raw dump, `GET /api/coredump/summary` gives the summary, and `DELETE /api/coredump` erases the dump. `/api/status` has `core_dump` (`present`, `state`, `size`). The Status tab of the web UI shows a line with **Download** and **Erase** while a dump is in flash.
- **Shell:** `coredump` gives the summary, `coredump dump` prints the full dump in base64 between two marker lines, and `coredump erase` erases it (a write command, `CONFIG_GW_SHELL_WRITE_COMMANDS`). A technician with a laptop at the panel can get the dump without the network.

`docs/how-to/decode-a-core-dump.md` tells how to decode the file with `esp-coredump` and the ELF of the same build. Keep the ELF of each firmware that you install: without it, nobody can decode the dump.

- **State and summary.** `gw_coredump_init()` reads the state at boot, before the other tasks start: is a dump present, its size, and is its checksum correct (`esp_core_dump_image_check`, one read of the dump). After that, only an erase changes the state. The summary (`esp_core_dump_get_summary` and `esp_core_dump_get_panic_reason`: the task, the PC, the backtrace, the exception cause and address, the panic reason) maps the partition. Thus only a request for the summary reads it, not `/api/status`. The summary also gives the reset reason of this boot; the dump can be older than this boot. The summary of a corrupt dump has the state only.
- **Memory.** Nothing holds a buffer of the size of the dump (up to 64 KB). The download sends pieces of 2 KB, and `coredump dump` reads pieces of 912 B (16 lines of 57 bytes). The request allocates these buffers and the summary (about 300 B) from the heap (PSRAM when present), and releases them at the end. The state uses no allocation.
- **Security.** The dump is a copy of the RAM at the crash. It can contain secrets in clear text: the MQTT password, TLS session keys and the private key of the device certificate. Therefore all `/api/coredump` routes need the web password in all modes, also in recovery mode. `DELETE` has the CSRF check of all writes. The shell asks for no password, because the console needs physical access, and that access also reads all of the flash through USB (§6.12). Erase the dump after a download, and keep the file as safe as the passwords of the gateway.
- **Concurrency.** A mutex serializes the reads, the summary and the erase. If an erase occurs during a download, the next read fails, and the download stops incomplete (the connection closes before the last chunk).

Verification in QEMU:

- the `panic` shell command (only in the emulator build) causes an `abort()`;
- the next boot reports `panic`;
- `esp_coredump info_corefile` decodes the dump in the partition, with the trace through `panic_task` and `abort`. §4.3 gives a limitation about the other core in QEMU;
- issue #132: the NBIRTH after the panic has `Node Info/Core Dump` = `true`. `GET /api/coredump` gives the bytes of the partition, and `esp_coredump` decodes them. The summary gives the task `panic`, the PC, the backtrace and the `abort()` reason. The base64 lines of `coredump dump` give the same bytes, and `esp_coredump --core-format b64` decodes them. `DELETE` without the CSRF header gets `403`. With the header, it erases the dump: an NDATA with `false`, then `404`.

Nobody tested this on the board.

### 3.3 Update an existing `sdkconfig` (migration note)

`sdkconfig.defaults` applies only when you **create** the `sdkconfig`. With a `sdkconfig` from an earlier version:

- **Before the declarative description of the board (`gw_bsp`):** `reconfigure` discards 17 pin options **without a warning** (6 from `gw_board`, 7 from `gw_net` and 4 from `gw_rs485`). The pins are now fixed in `gw_bsp.c`. Also, `CONFIG_GW_DO_ACTIVE_LOW=y` appears. This option **inverts the polarity of the DOs** compared with a build that was already tested.
- **Options added later** do not get into an existing `sdkconfig`: `CONFIG_UART_ISR_IN_IRAM`, the core 0 affinity of lwIP and esp-mqtt (`CONFIG_LWIP_TCPIP_TASK_AFFINITY_CPU0`, `CONFIG_MQTT_USE_CORE_0`), `CONFIG_MBEDTLS_DYNAMIC_BUFFER` (#80: without it and without PSRAM, `PUT /api/config` responds 503). Measured with IDF v6.1 (kconfgen 3.14): IDF **does not do the configuration again** when `sdkconfig.defaults` changes. Thus `idf.py build` continued with the old `sdkconfig`. When the configuration step runs, kconfgen applies the new default only to the values that it marked `# default:`. It does not apply it to a value from `menuconfig`, or to a file written without these marks.

After an update, the simplest procedure is to **regenerate the `sdkconfig`**: `rm sdkconfig && idf.py reconfigure` (or `idf.py set-target esp32s3`). For a different build directory, delete its `sdkconfig` and use the same `-B`/`-D SDKCONFIG=`/`-D SDKCONFIG_DEFAULTS=` again. For example, for the QEMU build: `rm build-qemu/sdkconfig && idf.py -B build-qemu -D SDKCONFIG=build-qemu/sdkconfig -D "SDKCONFIG_DEFAULTS=sdkconfig.defaults;sdkconfig.qemu" reconfigure`. If you keep the old `sdkconfig`, examine at least `CONFIG_GW_DO_ACTIVE_LOW` and the options above in `menuconfig`. No gateways are in service, thus the project did not add an automatic migration.

**Check in the build (#83).** The defaults files of the build are now dependencies of the configuration. When one of them changes, the next `idf.py build` does the configuration again. The values marked `# default:` then get the new default automatically (tested: `# CONFIG_MBEDTLS_DYNAMIC_BUFFER is not set` changed to `=y`).

Also, during the configuration, `cmake/gw_sdkconfig_check.cmake` compares the `sdkconfig` in use with the defaults chain of that build. The chain is `sdkconfig.defaults` plus the *overlays* of `SDKCONFIG_DEFAULTS` and their `.esp32s3` variants; the last one wins. The check covers a short list of options that the firmware needs:

- `MBEDTLS_DYNAMIC_BUFFER`, `MBEDTLS_CUSTOM_MEM_ALLOC`, `UART_ISR_IN_IRAM`;
- the *task watchdog* options (`ESP_TASK_WDT_EN`, `_INIT`, `_TIMEOUT_S`, `_PANIC`, `_CHECK_IDLE_TASK_CPU0/1`);
- `BOOTLOADER_APP_ROLLBACK_ENABLE` (FW-1);
- `ESP_COREDUMP_ENABLE_TO_FLASH`;
- `SPIRAM` and `SPIRAM_IGNORE_NOTFOUND` (#135: the firmware assumes the PSRAM, and a board without it boots only to report the fault);
- `FATFS_LFN_NONE` (#122).

If one of them is different, **the configuration fails**. The error gives the list (`CONFIG_MBEDTLS_DYNAMIC_BUFFER: sdkconfig has n, the defaults y`) and the procedure to regenerate. An *overlay* that changes one of these options on purpose does not cause the error, because the check compares with the overlay value. The check does not compare an option that is not in the `sdkconfig`. This occurs when the dependencies of the option are off, for example `SPIRAM_IGNORE_NOTFOUND` in the QEMU build without PSRAM. For a deliberate local change, use `idf.py -D GW_SDKCONFIG_CHECK=warn …`: it only gives a warning.

CI generates the `sdkconfig` from zero and does not see this problem. Host tests: `sdkconfig_check_*`. They cover these cases: current, old, old with `warn`, a different *watchdog* with and without an *overlay*. The checks of the production *overlay* (issues #93 and #108 B7) are on the branch `archive/efuse-production` (§3.7). Before #93, the tests with an *overlay* gave the list with `\;`, and the check never read the *overlay*. Thus `wdt5_overlay` passed and compared nothing.

**Prohibited combinations (issue #103):** the check also compares the `sdkconfig` with itself. `CONFIG_NVS_ENCRYPTION=y` without `CONFIG_SECURE_FLASH_ENC_ENABLED` fails outside the emulator build (`CONFIG_GW_IO_SIMULATED`). The reason: the copy from an NVS migration (§6.2.1) would leave the secrets in clear text in the free blocks of LittleFS. `warn` also lets this combination pass. Tests: `sdkconfig_check_nvsenc_noflash` (fails), `_warn` (gives a warning) and `sdkconfig_check_nvsenc_sim` (passes). Flash encryption is not in `main` (§3.7). Thus a build for the board with NVS encryption passes the check only with `warn`.

### 3.4 Configuration

1. The firmware embeds `components/gw_storage/default_config.json`. This file is the example of §6.
2. To replace it, copy the JSON to `idf/fs/config.json` and edit it. `idf.py flash` flashes the `fs/` folder into the `storage` partition. The firmware reads `/cfg/config.json` at boot.
3. `docs/SPEC.md` §6.1 describes the optional keys outside the MVP:
   - `network.wifi`, NET-4;
   - `network.ipv6`, NET-7;
   - `local_io.failsafe_s` and `do_failsafe`, IO-3;
   - `local_io.counter_save_s`, IO-4;
   - `local_io.buzzer`, IO-6;
   - `local_io.rules`, IO-7;
   - `mqtt.tls`, MQTT-4/5;
   - `mqtt.backup_brokers`, MQTT-6;
   - `mqtt.protocol`, MQTT-8;
   - `sparkplug.primary_host_id`, SPB-13;
   - `sparkplug.device_prefix`, the location levels before each device name (#172);
   - `sparkplug.naming`, the name checks and the UMH preset (#169);
   - `sparkplug.store_forward`, SPB-14;
   - `syslog`, REL-4;
   - `modbus_tcp`, MB-15;
   - 64-bit types, `string`, byte orders and `bit` in the tags, MB-6.
4. **The firmware uses the embedded configuration only if `/cfg/config.json` does not exist** (and no factory reset occurred, §3.5). All other problems put the gateway in **recovery mode**: the LittleFS partition does not mount, or the file is empty, cannot be read, is larger than the limit or is not valid. Then **the polling and MQTT do not start**. The network starts, with the network of the last valid configuration or with DHCP on Ethernet. The web UI, the shell and the access point (NET-5) also start, so you can correct the configuration without a new flash. The LED blinks red, and every 10 s the log gives the cause again with level ERROR. For example:
   - `/cfg/config.json: 140000 bytes; el máximo es 131072 (CONFIG_GW_CONFIG_MAX_BYTES)`;
   - `partición LittleFS 'storage' no montable (ESP_FAIL)`;
   - `devices[0].tags[1].writable: solo las áreas coil y holding son escribibles`.

   Semantic errors include the **JSON path** of the value (`devices[1].tags[2].type: valor no válido (se esperaba bool|uint16|…)`, `devices[0].tags[0].scal: clave desconocida`). Syntax errors include the **line and column**. The firmware counts them in characters from the error pointer of cJSON (`JSON mal formado en la línea 5, columna 3: sintaxis no válida`). The firmware also rejects content after the end of the document. It rejects **duplicate keys** in all objects (`mqtt.host: clave duplicada`): cJSON accepts them, and its searches see only the first one.
5. **Size limit: 128 KiB** (`CONFIG_GW_CONFIG_MAX_BYTES`, menu `Gateway: storage`). The maximum of the schema (16 devices, 256 tags with all fields) is approximately 46 KB in compact JSON and 82 KB with an indent of 2 spaces. At boot, the firmware allocates the file buffer and the cJSON tree (approximately 200 KB for 256 tags), **in PSRAM**. It releases them before it starts the tasks. The log gives the free heap before the parse (`/cfg/config.json: N bytes; free heap: internal … (largest block …), PSRAM …`).
6. **In the QEMU build without PSRAM** (`CONFIG_GW_EMU_NO_PSRAM`, §1.3), all memory comes from the internal heap. A board without PSRAM does not load the configuration (the fault mode, §1.3). Measured in QEMU, with approximately 315 KB of free internal heap at load:

   | Configuration (16 devices, tags with all fields) | File | Result |
   |---|---|---|
   | 256 tags (maximum of the schema), compact JSON | 46 052 B | Loads |
   | 256 tags, indent of 2 spaces | 82 450 B | Loads |
   | 256 tags, with padding spaces | up to 115 877 B | Loads |
   | 256 tags, with padding spaces | from 117 396 B | Rejected: `memoria insuficiente para analizar … bytes de JSON` |

   Thus, in this build, all legal configurations load **if the file is not larger than approximately 110 KB** (fewer tags give a little more margin). With PSRAM (the board), the only limit is `CONFIG_GW_CONFIG_MAX_BYTES`.

7. **Change the configuration without a new flash:** use the web UI (`https://<ip>/` or `https://<hostname>.local/`; `http://` redirects), the REST API (`PUT /api/config`, SPEC §6.1) or the shell (`config load`, then paste the JSON). In the three cases, the validation is the same. The firmware saves the configuration atomically (temporary file, `fsync`, `rename`), and the gateway does an orderly restart (NDEATH). `config show` and `GET /api/config` export the configuration with the secrets masked.

Build options in `idf.py menuconfig`, in the `Gateway: ...` menus:

- the polarity of the DIs and of the DOs (the pins are fixed, in `gw_bsp`);
- the priorities and the cores of the tasks;
- block reads and the maximum gap (default values: each device can set its own values with `block_reads` and `max_gap`);
- forced FC15/FC16;
- the maximum interval between probes of an offline device (`GW_MB_OFFLINE_PROBE_MAX_MS`);
- the MQTT backoff;
- Wi-Fi compiled or not (`CONFIG_GW_WIFI`, menu `Gateway: network`; without it, the firmware ignores `network.wifi` and gives a warning);
- the SNTP wait time;
- the number of lines that the remote syslog keeps (`CONFIG_GW_SYSLOG_SLOTS`, 32 by default) and the priority of its task;
- the queue sizes;
- the Modbus TCP gateway: simultaneous clients, priority and the maximum wait for a bus response (menu `Gateway: Modbus TCP gateway`);
- the shell commands that change something (`CONFIG_GW_SHELL_WRITE_COMMANDS`), the access point (`CONFIG_GW_SOFTAP`, minutes after BOOT) and mDNS (`CONFIG_GW_MDNS`).

**Status LED** (from the highest to the lowest priority):

| State | Pattern |
|---|---|
| Factory reset in progress | White, continuous |
| BOOT pushed for more than 2 s | White, blinks at 4 Hz |
| Commissioning (no configuration after a reset) | Blue, blinks at 1 Hz |
| Configuration not valid (recovery mode) | Red, blinks at 4 Hz |
| No network (no IP) | Red, continuous |
| No broker | Amber, blinks at 1 Hz |
| Modbus device offline | Green and orange, alternately (1 Hz) |
| Normal operation | Green, continuous |

### 3.5 Commissioning, factory reset and secrets

**Access.** The web UI and the API use **HTTPS** (`web.https`, the default) with a certificate that is specific to the gateway. The gateway generates this certificate at the first boot (and after a factory reset) and keeps it with the secrets in NVS. The certificate is self-signed, thus the browser gives a warning the first time. The SHA-256 fingerprint that the browser shows must be the same as the fingerprint on the console at each boot (`web: HTTPS certificate SHA-256 fingerprint AB:CD:…`). HTTP (port 80) only redirects.

The web UI and the API require HTTP Basic with `web.username` and `web.password`. After 5 failures in sequence from one address, the gateway blocks that address for 30 s (then 60, 120… up to 15 min). While there is no web password, use the user `admin` and the **commissioning password**. This password has 12 random characters and is different for each gateway. The gateway creates it at the first boot and keeps it in NVS. The console shows it at each boot (`web: no web password set; user 'admin', commissioning password '…'`; with `printf`, never through syslog).

In recovery mode and in commissioning, the saved web password is also valid, with any user (the user name is in the rejected document).

**Procedure on site:**

1. Connect Ethernet (DHCP). If there is no network, use the access point.
   - When the configuration was rejected, or in commissioning, the access point starts automatically.
   - With a valid configuration, push BOOT for 2 to 10 s. The access point then starts for 30 min (`CONFIG_GW_SOFTAP_TIMEOUT_MIN`).
   - The network is `GW-Setup-XXXXXX` (the last bytes of the MAC), WPA2 with the commissioning password. The web UI is at `https://192.168.4.1/`.
2. Open `https://<ip>/` (or `https://<hostname>.local/`, NET-6). Compare the fingerprint with the fingerprint on the console. Then accept the certificate of the gateway. Load or edit the configuration (the forms, the JSON tab or Importar). Click Validar, then click Guardar. The gateway restarts with the new configuration.
3. Set your own `web.password` (at least 8 characters).

**Factory reset (CFG-5):** push BOOT for 10 s while the gateway operates. GPIO0 is a *strapping* pin: if you push it at power-on, the ROM goes into download mode. Thus the firmware reads the button only after boot, and it must see the button released one time. At 2 s, the LED changes to white. At 10 s, the gateway gives two beeps and erases the full LittleFS partition and NVS (configuration, secrets, commissioning password). Then the gateway does an orderly restart in **commissioning mode**.

In commissioning mode, the gateway does not use the embedded example configuration or its Sparkplug identity. It waits for a configuration from the web UI or the shell, with a new commissioning password. In QEMU, `boot 1` / `boot 0` simulates the button.

**Secrets (CFG-7):** `mqtt.password`, `network.wifi.password` and the hash of `web.password` (PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, 2048 iterations, random salt) are in NVS, never in `/cfg/config.json`:

| Build | How | NVS | eFuses |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Default** (the build for the board) | `idf.py set-target esp32s3 build` | **Not encrypted**: the secrets are not in the JSON, but a person can read them from a dump of the flash. The shell gives the warning `secrets  NVS NOT encrypted` | Burns no eFuse: you can flash it on any board, and flash it again |
| **QEMU** (E2E only) | `sdkconfig.defaults;sdkconfig.qemu` (`tools/e2e/run_e2e.sh`) | Encrypted with the HMAC scheme (`CONFIG_NVS_ENCRYPTION`, `CONFIG_NVS_SEC_KEY_PROTECT_USING_HMAC`, key in the eFuse block **KEY5**). The shell shows `secrets  NVS encrypted (HMAC key in eFuse KEY5)` | Burns KEY5 in the emulated eFuse file of each run. Nothing real is burned |

The project does not encrypt NVS on the board (decision of #133, §3.7). The HMAC key in eFuse is permanent, and the owner of the board must keep full control of it. The QEMU build keeps the NVS encryption and its migration code until #133 decides about them. Outside the emulator build, the build check rejects NVS encryption without flash encryption (§3.3, issue #103).

- **Migration.** The firmware accepts a `config.json` with secrets in clear text (flashed with `idf.py flash`, or from an earlier firmware). At boot, the secrets move to NVS. The firmware **erases the full LittleFS partition** (LittleFS is *copy-on-write*, and the old blocks would keep the text). Then it writes the file again without the secrets. During this window, the gateway has a commissioning mark: a power cut does not leave it with the example identity.

  Issues #67 and #94 cover the change from a build without NVS encryption to a build with NVS encryption. The first encrypted boot identifies the unencrypted NVS from its raw pages (`gw_nvsraw`: the CRC of the entries in clear text; it initializes nothing). It **moves all the entries** to the encrypted NVS: all the namespaces (secrets, commissioning password, web certificate, I/O state). This move is **safe from power cuts** (issue #94):

  1. The firmware reads the keys of the encrypted NVS. At the first encrypted boot, it generates them (this burns KEY5). If this step fails, it changes nothing, and that boot continues with the unencrypted NVS.
  2. It copies all the entries to `/cfg/.nvsmig` in LittleFS (temporary file, `fsync` and `rename`; LittleFS is not encrypted, see §6.2.1). **It erases the NVS only if all the entries are in the copy** (`gw_nvsmig_may_erase`). In these cases, it erases nothing, and that boot continues without encryption:
     - the unencrypted NVS does not start because of an error that is not a format error (`ESP_ERR_NO_MEM`, a flash read error…);
     - there is not sufficient memory to read a value;
     - the list of entries stops before its end;
     - one entry was not copied.

     Exception: with `NO_FREE_PAGES` or `NEW_VERSION_FOUND`, IDF erases the partition in all cases. Only then does the migration continue with the available data. If LittleFS cannot mount, there is no copy, and the firmware erases nothing.
  3. It erases the partition and initializes it as encrypted. It writes the entries, reads them again and compares them. At the end, it writes a mark (`gw/nvsmig_ok`) with the CRC of the completed copy.
  4. It deletes the copy, and then the mark.

  A power cut at any point in step 3 leaves the copy. The next boot finds the copy and **continues** from that point (`NVS migration was interrupted …: writing its copy`, then `NVS migration resumed: N entries moved into the encrypted NVS`). A cut between step 3 and step 4 leaves the copy and the mark. Then the firmware deletes only the copy, and only if the mark has **its** CRC. An old copy never goes onto a newer encrypted NVS.

  The firmware deletes a copy **only if it read the full copy and the copy fails the check** (or is not a copy). It keeps the copy if it cannot read it (no memory, an error at open, or a short read). It also keeps a copy in a format that this firmware does not know. In these cases, that boot continues without NVS (it erases, opens and initializes nothing), because the copy can be the only copy of the entries. The firmware reads version 1 of the copy (from ebc2aa4, 16-byte header) and converts it.

  A cut can occur before the erase is complete (pages in clear text remain). Then the next boot reads the partition again and uses the copy to complete the entries that are not there now. The entries in the partition are the newest. During a resume, the encrypted NVS can fail to start because of an error that is not a format error. Then the firmware changes nothing, and that boot has no NVS. The reason: this can be a completed migration that is newer than the copy.

  If a raw page cannot be read (or there is no memory to read it), the firmware cannot classify the partition. Then it migrates nothing and erases nothing. If all the readable pages are encrypted, it opens the encrypted NVS. If all of them are in clear text, it continues without encryption (the entries in clear text are the newest, as in `MIXED`). That boot has **no NVS** in these cases: no page was read, there are pages of the two types, or there is a pending copy. The firmware never initializes in clear text a partition that can be encrypted.

  **General rule:** when the boot cannot know the contents of the partition, it runs without NVS. It also runs without NVS when there is a pending copy that it will not apply. Then there are no saved secrets: with an MQTT password, the gateway does not connect. The commissioning password and the web certificate are new and in RAM, and the firmware does not save them. The firmware never runs on a partition that is half erased, thus no regenerated value can later win over the copy.

  The shell tells this: `secrets  NVS UNAVAILABLE this boot: <motivo>`. If the encrypted write fails two times in sequence, the copy stays for the next boot. That boot continues without NVS (no secrets).

  The normal log is `NVS migration: N entries moved into the encrypted NVS (0 skipped)`. During a resume, it is `NVS migration resumed: N entries moved into the encrypted NVS (0 skipped)`. The copy records how many entries it did not copy; with the check above, this number is always 0.

  The NVS has no bdSeq since issue #161: the bdSeq is in RAM and starts at 0 at each boot (§1.2). Thus a boot without NVS, a migration and an NVS erase (`NVS layout changed, erasing`) do not change the bdSeq. Before #161, the NVS kept the bdSeq in `spb/bdseq`, and a boot without NVS reserved values in `/cfg/.nvsmig-bdseq`. The firmware does not read these old data: an old `spb/bdseq` entry stays in the NVS with no use (a migration moves it as any other entry), and an old reservation file stays in LittleFS with no use. A factory reset erases both.

  **A new image on trial (FW-1) does not migrate, and safe mode does not migrate (#144).** If the *rollback* returns to an image without encryption, that image must find the NVS as it left it. The image on trial uses the unencrypted NVS. A copy can exist from a migration that stopped before the erase was complete. Then the image on trial runs **without NVS** (the partition is half erased), and the copy stays. When the firmware confirms the image, it also restarts, and the boot that migrates completes the partition with the copy.

  The trial does not need a session (issue #144: the self-check confirms the image). Until the confirmation, the log shows `NVS partition "nvs" is not encrypted and stays so this boot: this firmware is on trial …`, and the shell shows `secrets  NVS NOT encrypted yet`. When the firmware confirms the image, the gateway does one orderly restart (`fw: firmware … confirmed: restarting once to encrypt the NVS`), and that boot migrates.

  The firmware treats a partition with pages in clear text and encrypted pages (`GW_NVSRAW_MIXED`) as unencrypted. Only a build without encryption writes entries in clear text. At start, this build erases the encrypted entries that it cannot read. Thus the entries in clear text are the newest.

  The opposite direction (from encrypted to unencrypted) has no migration: the build without encryption cannot read the encrypted NVS. Thus an image without encryption that an OTA installs on a gateway that already migrated loses the NVS. This is a version downgrade, not a *rollback*.

### 3.6 Firmware updates (FW-1)

Issue #144 gives the process of an update and of a rollback. The product owner approved it on 7 Nov 2026. The decision logic is pure C with host tests (`gw_core`: `gw_ota`, `gw_bootloop`, `gw_fwlast`, `gw_cfgschema`, `gw_lkgnet`). `gw_fw` applies it with `CONFIG_BOOTLOADER_APP_ROLLBACK_ENABLE`.

**How an update starts:**

- **NCMD** `Node Control/Firmware Update` (String, alias 40) with the `https://…` URL of the image (`build/rs485_spb_gateway.bin`; the QEMU build uses a signed image, §3.7). The Sparkplug codec accepts texts of up to 256 characters, and an update URL can have up to 255 characters (issue #87). The firmware rejects a longer URL and gives the cause. It never truncates the URL.
- **Web:** the "Firmware" card of the status page, or the API: `POST /api/firmware` with `{"url": "https://…"}` (`application/json`, up to 255 characters) or with the image as the body (`application/octet-stream`). A different `Content-Type` gives 415. As for all writes, the request must have `X-Requested-With` (issue #100, SPEC §6.1). `GET /api/firmware` gives the two slots, the state, the progress, the trial and the boot-loop counter.
- **The other slot:** the button "Restart into `<version>`", `POST /api/firmware/other` or the NCMD `Node Control/Boot Other Slot` (Boolean `true`, alias 43). See "The other slot" below.
- `ota.enabled: false` (SPEC §6.1) rejects all of these. In recovery mode, in safe mode and in commissioning, the web UI can always upload an image.

**How the download operates:**

- The download runs in a dedicated task (`fw_update`, 8 KB, priority 2, core 0) that exists only during the download. The Sparkplug task does not block. The firmware writes the flash sector by sector as the data arrives (`esp_https_ota`; it does not erase the full 4 MB at one time).
- Only `https://`. The CA of the server is `ota.ca_file`. If it is not set, the firmware uses `mqtt.tls.ca_file` (with TLS active). If that is not set, it uses the public CA bundle of ESP-IDF. To verify the certificate, the firmware needs the time (SNTP).
- The image must be from this project (the `project_name` of its `esp_app_desc_t`). The firmware checks this before it writes past the header. Then it does the pre-flight check (below). `esp_ota_end()` verifies the full image: the checksum and the SHA-256. The default build does not require a signature. The QEMU build also verifies the signature (§3.7).
- If a step fails, the running firmware continues, and the state gives the cause. If the image is valid, the firmware writes the record of the update (`fw_last`, below) and sets the boot partition. Then the gateway publishes `restarting into <version>` and does an orderly restart (NDEATH).

**The modes of the gateway.** A mode is what the boot reached:

- **normal:** the configuration is valid, and the gateway runs.
- **recovery:** nothing runs but the network, the web UI, the shell and the firmware upload. It has a cause: the configuration was rejected, or repeated crashes (safe mode), or no memory (no PSRAM, the fault mode of #135).
- **commissioning:** after a factory reset, there is no configuration yet (CFG-5).

**The trial of a new image (item 1).** A new image boots on trial (`ESP_OTA_IMG_PENDING_VERIFY`). The trial is a **self-check of the gateway**. It does not use the broker, the Modbus slaves, NTP or the update server.

- The firmware confirms the image at the first instant when all of these are true:
  - the uptime is 60 s or more (`CONFIG_GW_FW_SELFCHECK_S`). The uptime comes from the FreeRTOS tick of this boot: in QEMU the system timer of `esp_timer` does not restart at a software reset;
  - the gateway is in its expected mode (item 2, below);
  - the network driver started (not the link, not the broker);
  - the loop of the web server answered the ping of `web_guard` in the last 5 s. This item applies only when a web server runs (normal mode with `web.enabled`, recovery mode, commissioning).
- To confirm the image is to write `otadata` (`esp_ota_mark_app_valid_cancel_rollback()`). The `main` task does the check about one time per second, after `app_main` reached its mode. If the write fails, the image stays on trial. The firmware tries the write again after 1, 2, 4 … up to 60 s, and one more time before a planned restart (issue #99).
- **Backstop:** if the self-check is not true at 5 min (`CONFIG_GW_FW_BACKSTOP_S`), the firmware rejects the image with the cause `self-check failed: <item>`. The task watchdog does not watch a blocked `app_main`: thus an `esp_timer` rejects the image 10 s after the backstop if the check did not run.
- **Rejection:** the firmware writes the cause to `fw_last`, then does a planned restart (NDEATH, the flush of #136). The image is still pending, so the bootloader boots the earlier image. The firmware does not call `esp_ota_mark_app_invalid_rollback_and_reboot()`, so the app does not verify the earlier image. Thus the `fw_trial` task of #108, the special code of #108 B4/B6, #109 and #118, and the QEMU check of the rollback stack (#123) are removed.
- **Any reset during the trial** (crash, watchdog, power cut, a restart) makes the bootloader boot the earlier image.
