A command that needs a part that did not start yet tells you (`mbread: not available yet: the RS485 bus is starting`; also `ping`, `rebirth`, `di`). `status`, `devices` and `config` tell you that the configuration is loading. Text that you type before the application starts (bootloader) is still lost.
- The shell does not ask for a password. Access to the console requires physical access (USB-C), the same access that the BOOT button and the flash require. `CONFIG_GW_SHELL_WRITE_COMMANDS=n` gives a read-only console.
- `config load` reads the pasted document until its outer object closes. It stops after 60 s without data. The console read has no echo. While you paste, the shell does not take the lock of the shell context (issue #78). Thus the boot (which updates that context) and the tasks that report that their subsystem is up continue. The shell takes the lock only to validate, save and restart, with the context of that moment.
- `mbread` goes through the bus queue (single owner, MB-16): it waits until the current transaction ends. A device that does not exist gives `timeout` or `framing error` (this depends on the answers of the rest of the bus).
- **The access point (NET-5) is compile-only** (QEMU has no radio). There is no captive portal: open `https://192.168.4.1/`. With Wi-Fi STA configured, the driver changes to AP+STA, and the AP uses the channel of the STA.
- **mDNS (NET-6) is compile-only for the responses:** in QEMU the responder starts, but `open_eth` has no multicast filter, and the responder does not use that interface. The W5500 has a filter (`add_mac_filter`), but nobody verified it on the board.
- The factory reset and the migration erase the full 1 MB LittleFS partition. On real flash, this takes some seconds (in 64 KB steps).
- The gateway decrypts the secrets of the encrypted NVS to RAM at boot. A memory dump (a *core dump* in flash) can contain them. `GET /api/coredump` and the shell's `coredump dump` give the dump (issue #132, §3.2): the web routes need the password, the shell needs physical access. Keep a downloaded dump as safe as the passwords, and erase it from the gateway after the download.
- **Migration of NVS to encrypted NVS (issue #94).** The window without the entries in the NVS does not lose them now. They are in the copy `/cfg/.nvsmig` until the gateway wrote the encrypted NVS and read it again. That window is not «tens of ms». It includes the key generation and the burn of KEY5 (first encrypted boot), the erase of 24 KB, and all writes with their `commit`. In QEMU, the window is some seconds. Limits:
  - The copy holds the secrets in plain text in LittleFS. Flash encryption (FW-3) is not in `main` (§3.7). In a build with NVS encryption but without flash encryption, the blocks of the deleted file stay in the partition until LittleFS uses them again. **That combination does not configure now** outside the emulator build (issue #103): `gw_sdkconfig_check` rejects it (`CONFIG_NVS_ENCRYPTION=y without CONFIG_SECURE_FLASH_ENC_ENABLED …`). `-D GW_SDKCONFIG_CHECK=warn` permits it intentionally, for example on a development board. Zeros written over the file before the delete would not help: LittleFS is *copy-on-write*, and the new write goes to new blocks. The QEMU build still has this combination: the E2E checks that no plain text stays in the `nvs` partition, but not in `storage`.
  - Without a LittleFS that can mount (a partition that was never formatted), the migration does not occur. The NVS stays unencrypted until a boot with LittleFS (a configuration save formats LittleFS).
  - If the gateway cannot write the encrypted NVS two times in sequence, that boot continues without NVS (no secrets: MQTT with a password does not connect). The next boot tries again from the copy.
  - An image on trial keeps the NVS unencrypted until the gateway confirms the image. Then the gateway restarts one time (one more NDEATH/NBIRTH). Until then, the secrets stay as the previous image left them: unencrypted. The gateway creates the *timer* of that restart when it boots on trial, not in the Sparkplug task that confirms the image (REL-2, issue #104). If the NVS stayed unencrypted because LittleFS does not mount, the next boot could not do the migration either: the gateway does not restart (the log tells this).
  - The gateway **rejects** an image without NVS encryption that arrives by OTA on a gateway that did the migration (issue #102, §3.6). If you flash such an image through USB, the NVS is lost.
  - Only the host tests examine the error paths that destroy nothing (no memory, a read error of the flash or of the copy, a cut listing, an unreadable raw page). They test the pure decision (`gw_nvsmig_decide`, `gw_nvsmig_may_erase`), because QEMU cannot inject these errors. In QEMU, the power cuts and the *rollback* run.
  - **Unreadable copy = no NVS at each boot.** Sometimes `/cfg/.nvsmig` exists, but the gateway can never read it (damaged LittleFS blocks, a format from a newer firmware). Then the gateway always boots without NVS:
    - no stored secrets (MQTT with a password does not connect);
    - a new commissioning password at each boot.

    The shell tells this (`secrets  NVS UNAVAILABLE this boot: the copy of an NVS migration (/cfg/.nvsmig) cannot be read …`). The solution is a **factory reset** (BOOT 10 s, §3.5). It erases all of LittleFS (the copy, the configuration and the certificates) and the NVS (secrets, commissioning password, web certificate). The gateway goes back to commissioning. The entries that were only in the copy are lost. An image with a newer copy format, after a rollback by OTA, gets into this state.
  - **LittleFS that does not mount after a cut (issue #104).** The migration writes a start mark (`nvsmig_busy`) before any entry, and deletes it together with the end mark. Sometimes LittleFS does not mount and its partition is not blank (the copy can be there). Then these NVS states mean **no NVS in that boot**:
    - an empty NVS with the keys already generated (a cut immediately after the erase);
    - a plain NVS, or a mixed NVS, with the keys and the «erase in progress» entry (a cut during the erase, issues #108 B2 and #109);
    - an encrypted NVS with the start mark and without the end mark (a cut in the middle of the writes).

    The gateway does not open an empty or half-written encrypted NVS, because the resume would later discard its new entries. The shell tells this (`secrets  NVS UNAVAILABLE this boot: LittleFS does not mount and the NVS looks like an interrupted migration …`). A `storage` partition with an erased superblock counts as blank (a readable copy cannot be there).

    **In that state the gateway does not format LittleFS** (issue #108 M1), because the only copy can be there. The gateway rejects a configuration save (`configuración no guardada: LittleFS no monta y puede tener la copia de una migración de NVS (/cfg/.nvsmig); formatearlo la perdería`). Before, that save formatted LittleFS and lost the secrets. The real solutions are:
    - A boot in which LittleFS mounts again (the migration completes from the copy).
    - **Discard the copy with an intentional configuration save** (issue #109). Use `config load discard-nvs-copy` in the shell or `PUT /api/config?discard_nvs_copy=1` in the API. The value must be exactly `1`, as for all the API options. This formats LittleFS and saves the document (the log says `configuration saved discarding the unreachable NVS migration copy`). The data that was only in the copy is lost: the secrets (MQTT and Wi-Fi passwords, the *hash* of the web password, the commissioning password, the web certificate). The NVS stays as the cut left it (empty or half-written). The gateway rejects a document with new secrets in that boot (there is no NVS): save the document without them, and load them later.
    - A **factory reset** (BOOT 10 s), which also erases the NVS.

    **«Plain with keys» is not ambiguous now (issue #109).** Immediately before the erase of the partition, the migration writes and commits an entry `gw/_gw_erase_mark` (u8 `0xA5`) in the plain NVS. It erases the page of this entry last. The gateway identifies the entry by itself, with any namespace index. The reason: the page with the entry of the `gw` namespace is usually older, and the erase removes it first (issue #109, second review; the E2E fills page 0 with `gw` to test this). If the migration wrote the entry and the gateway cannot find it, the erase removed the page of the entry. The erase removes that page last, thus no other page remains to erase.

    A plain NVS that does not even start because of its format (full: `NO_FREE_PAGES`, or `NEW_VERSION_FOUND`) has nothing readable to save. The gateway erases all of it without the entry, as IDF would do. (Before this fix, the migration never occurred: `GW44` in the E2E.) Without that entry, the erase never started, and the partition is intact. Examples: keys generated and then no memory, a copy that the gateway could not write, or an entry that it could not copy. When LittleFS is not available, the boot continues with the plain NVS, as any boot without space for the copy. When LittleFS does not mount and an encrypted NVS does not even start, or would give `NO_FREE_PAGES`/`NEW_VERSION_FOUND`, the gateway also erases nothing: no NVS (issue #109 B-d).
  - **A plain page with more bad entries than good entries reads as encrypted (issue #97).** The rule is `plain_ok >= bad` per page, and it stays. A valid plain CRC on an encrypted entry is a 2^-32 chance. But a page read as plain next to encrypted pages gives `MIXED`, and the migration erases the encrypted pages. Thus a small number of matches cannot decide. A real plain NVS does not get to that state (NVS marks an entry as written only after it writes it): flash damage is necessary. If this occurs on all pages, the boot opens the encrypted NVS, and the old entries are lost, also the secrets and the bdSeq. Since issue #107, a string or *blob* with a damaged header that still reads as one item (type, *span* and declared size agree) counts as one bad item, not one per entry. Three of them on a page of eight items do not make the page encrypted now: more damaged items than good items are necessary (`test_more_bad_than_plain`, `test_damaged_multi_entry_items_count_once`). A damaged size still counts per entry.
  - The gateway resolves the mixed partition (`MIXED`) in favor of the plain entries. The remaining encrypted entries are lost. No firmware path showed this state: it is the mark of a build without encryption that wrote over an encrypted NVS.
- **Buzzer (IO-6):** LEDC at 2.7 kHz. Nobody verified the buzzer type or its resonance frequency (the demo uses 1 kHz). You cannot silence an alarm while its condition continues (the BOOT button already has its gestures). The `config_error` alarm depends on the request of the last valid configuration (NVS): on a gateway that never had one, the alarm does not sound. During a long flash erase (factory reset), the buzzer task stops. Thus the two beeps of the reset sound fully **before** the erase.
- **QEMU and restarts.** In the emulator, `esp_restart` does not reset UART1 or the interrupt matrix. If a Modbus response arrives during the restart, the next boot hangs in `uart_driver_install` (interrupt storm). The controlled restarts (REST, shell, NCMD Reboot, factory reset) make the bus silent first (`gw_bus_quiesce`). This also prevents a cut frame on the board.

### 6.3 Sparkplug behaviors that we selected

- **Diagnostics (SPB-17).** `Free Heap` is the internal RAM. The PSRAM, if present, does not count, because the internal RAM is the memory that becomes full. The per-device Modbus TCP counters (#69) go in a separate table, `Node Info/Modbus TCP` (alias 56, only with `modbus_tcp.enabled`, #81). This table has one more row for the units without a device. The table has up to 17 rows (`device_count + 1`). With many devices and a short `diagnostics_s`, the table uses up to approximately 1.6 KB per NDATA (measured with 16 rows).
- **Quality (SPB-16).** The gateway publishes a failed read (exception) as `is_null` with `Quality` = 0 (BAD) in DDATA. The next good read brings the value with `Quality` = 192 (GOOD). The DBIRTH has `Quality` in each metric. The DDATA messages have it only when it changes (a host that misses a DDATA gets the quality again with the next change or a rebirth). The gateway does not use STALE (500): a device that stops responding goes through DDEATH, not through old values. The gateway ignores the properties in an NCMD/DCMD. If a command has a property string of more than 63 characters, the gateway does not decode it and discards all of it (as for a long name).
- A device starts offline, and the gateway publishes its DBIRTH only after the first response.
- **A cycle fails if a request has no response after the retries** (a Modbus exception counts as a response). The cycle stops there and does not use the timeout on the remaining blocks. The failed cycles count for `offline_after`. Thus the DDEATH arrives in `offline_after` cycles, also when the other blocks of the device respond.
- The tags of the blocks that the gateway did not read **keep the value and the timestamp of their last read**: the gateway does not report them as fresh. If the problem continues, the DDEATH invalidates all of them. A device goes (or goes back) online only after a full cycle. Thus its DBIRTH never has values that the gateway did not read.
- **Probe with backoff for offline devices.** An offline device does not use the bus as a healthy device does now. Each probe sends **only the first request of the cycle, with one attempt** (no retries). The gateway schedules the next probe at 2, 4, 8… × `poll_ms`, to a maximum of `CONFIG_GW_MB_OFFLINE_PROBE_MAX_MS` (10 s by default; never less than `poll_ms`). If the device responds, the full cycle continues in that same pass. A device is offline in these cases:
  - a device that was online, after `offline_after` failed cycles in sequence (MB-9);
  - **a device that never responded since the boot, from its first cycle** (#61). For Sparkplug, this device is already offline (it had no DBIRTH). Thus it does not get full cycles with retries, and the backoff starts at its first failure. Before, with a number of absent devices, the bus was saturated for the first seconds (Meter1 got to a 2 s interval). The cost: if the first request is lost (noise, a slave that boots more slowly than the gateway), its DBIRTH arrives 2, 4… × `poll_ms` later, not one cycle later.

  **Limited return to online:** the DBIRTH arrives a maximum of one maximum interval plus one cycle after the device responds again (≤ 10 s + cycle by default). With the default values, a dead device uses the bus for 200 ms each 10 s, not 600 ms each second.
- The gateway discards a DCMD to an offline device. It does not put the DCMD in the queue.
- The gateway retries the writes as any other transaction. FC05/06/15/16 are idempotent, but a slave that executes the write and does not respond receives the write two times.
- **Primary host (SPB-13).** While the gateway waits for the STATE `online` of the host, the MQTT session is up (subscribed), but the node is not born. There is no NBIRTH or DDATA, and the gateway discards NCMD/DCMD (there is no Sparkplug session). With one broker, a host that never publishes its STATE makes the node wait forever. This is not an error: the specification requires it. The shell tells this (`host primary 'X' not seen yet, NBIRTH waits for it`). The gateway keeps the last accepted timestamp for each broker of the list, because the host keeps one STATE timestamp for each MQTT server (issue #157, `tck-id-operational-behavior-host-application-multi-server-timestamp`). These values are in RAM: after a restart, the gateway accepts any STATE. Only Sparkplug 3.0: the gateway ignores the `STATE/{id}` with `ONLINE`/`OFFLINE` of version 2.x.
- **bdSeq (SPB-3, issue #161).** The bdSeq starts at 0 at each boot and increases by exactly 1 for each MQTT CONNECT packet (`tck-id-topics-nbirth-bdseq-increment`); it is not in NVS. An attempt that sent no CONNECT packet (DNS, TCP or TLS failure, no client) does not use a value (§1.2). We selected this for an attempt with an unknown result (a CONNECT write that failed, no CONNACK): the attempt keeps its value, and the next CONNECT gets the next value. The broker can publish the *will* of such an attempt, and a host must not take it for the NDEATH of a newer session. After a restart, the bdSeq is 0 again, also when the session before the restart had 0. The broker publishes the *will* of the old connection when the new connection with the same `client_id` takes the session, thus before the new NBIRTH.
- **Failed births (issue #164).** If a birth publish fails while the MQTT connection stays open, the gateway ends the session. It publishes an NDEATH if the hosts saw the NBIRTH, and then it disconnects. The next attempt has a new CONNECT, a new bdSeq and all the births. This applies to the births of a new session and to a rebirth (NCMD, shell, SNTP). esp-mqtt 1.1.0 returns -1 for a QoS 0 publish only when the connection is closed or closes, but `gw_node` also fails a message that it cannot encode. The emulator-only shell command `pubfail <n>` makes the next publishes fail, for the E2E.
- **Spontaneous rebirth:** if the births went out without a valid time (no SNTP and no RTC time: 1970 timestamps), the gateway sends them again when SNTP synchronizes. Births with the RTC time get no rebirth (§6.4). The E2E did not run this branch.

- **MB-6:**
  - A write of a `bit` reads the register and writes all of it again. No part of the gateway can write between the read and the write (both go through the task that owns the bus). But if the slave itself changes a different bit of that register in that interval, the change is lost. The gateway does not use FC22 (*mask write*) on the slaves that have it.
  - `string` values have a maximum of 32 characters (16 registers) and only printable ASCII (other characters show as `?`). There is no UTF-8 or other encoding. The text goes as a Sparkplug String. For report by exception, the gateway compares a 64-bit FNV-1a hash (a collision would hide a change; the probability is negligible).
  - With `scale`/`offset`, an `int64`/`uint64` becomes a Double and is not exact above 2^53, as any scaled value.
  - With `scale`/`offset`, an `int32`/`uint32` also becomes a Double, not a Float (#165). A Float has a 24-bit mantissa, thus a 32-bit counter above 16 777 216 lost its last digits. A scaled `int16`, `uint16` or `float32` stays a Float. The store & forward record does not change, because it always keeps a scaled value as a double. But the datatype is part of the configuration hash of the microSD ring (§6.5, #145). Thus the first boot with this firmware starts a new ring when the configuration has a scaled 32-bit tag, and the old samples on the card are not published. No gateway is installed, so there is no migration.
  - **Memory:** the value of each tag now uses 56 B (24 B before) to hold the text. This is approximately 13 KB more of internal `.bss` (the process image of 256 tags, the working copy of the polling and the copy of the shell; §3.2). It is also 2 KB more in the Sparkplug engine (PSRAM if present).

- **MB-15 (Modbus TCP):**
  - **No authentication and no encryption:** anyone who gets to the port and is in `allowed_clients` reads and writes all slaves on the bus, also registers that the gateway does not poll. The list filters by source address (a device on the same network can make this address false). For this reason, the function is off by default.
  - Only IPv4: with IPv6 on (NET-7), the server does not listen on IPv6.
  - One transaction at a time for all clients; the other clients wait. A request waits a maximum of the current transaction and the pending DCMD writes. If the bus did not execute the request in 8 s (`CONFIG_GW_MBTCP_RESPONSE_WAIT_MS`), the client receives 0B.
  - No FC07, FC08, FC17, FC22, FC23 or FC43: exception 01. Also no *broadcast* (unit 0).
  - **Counters (#69):** each device counts separately the attempts of the TCP requests that the gateway forwards to its unit (`devices`: `| tcp ok … timeout … crc … frame … exception …`). The gateway counts these items: accepted and rejected connections, forwarded requests, the exceptions that it answered itself (01, 03, 0A, 0B), and the attempts to units without a configured device (the `modbus_tcp` line of `devices`). The per-device counters and the `(other units)` row go over Sparkplug in the DataSet `Node Info/Modbus TCP` (alias 56, #81). The connections, the forwarded requests and the exceptions that the gateway answers itself are only in the shell (`devices`).
  - Memory: 5.8 KB of `.bss` (4 KB stack, the buffers of 2 clients and the transaction, §3.2), also when the function is off.

### 6.3.0 Device prefix (#172), name checks and the UMH preset (#169, #173, #174)

- `sparkplug.device_prefix` holds the location levels that are the same for all devices of the gateway, for example `acme:berlin:packaging:line3`. The `device_id` of each device is `<device_prefix>:<name>` in each DBIRTH, DDATA, DDEATH and DCMD topic. A DCMD must use the full `device_id`. Without the key, the `device_id` is the name, as before.
- The key has no fixed number of levels. UMH Core makes the location path from the `device_id`, and it permits a path of free depth. The user writes the location one time, not in each of the 16 devices. The web UI shows the prefix as a list of levels that the user can add and remove.
- The engine writes the prefix into the topic buffer of the message (`GW_TOPIC_MAX`, 160 B). It keeps no copy of each `device_id`: there is no allocation in the publish path, and the Sparkplug engine does not become larger. The validation makes sure that the longest topic fits.
- Metric names do not get the prefix. The `Device` column of `Node Info/Modbus` and `Node Info/Modbus TCP` holds the device name without the prefix, because the prefix is the same for each row.
- The prefix is part of the configuration hash of the store & forward ring on the microSD card (§6.5, #145, format 2): a replay publishes on the topic of the `device_id`. Thus a new prefix starts a new ring, and the samples of the old ring are not published. This firmware also starts a new ring at its first boot. No gateway is installed, so there is no migration.
- **Name checks (#169).** `gw_config_lint()` (`gw_cfglint.c`) examines the parsed configuration for the names that UMH Core and other UNS hosts change with no error. The rules are in SPEC §6.1. It runs only at the validation, never in the publish path. It allocates nothing: it gives each finding to a callback.
  - `PUT /api/config` (also the dry run) sends the warnings of an accepted document in its answer: `warnings` (path and message, the first 50) and `warning_count`. The answer starts with the first warning and goes out in HTTP chunks, so no warning list stays in RAM. The lint runs after the save lock and the document are released.
  - The shell `config check` and `config load` give each warning as a `config: warning: <path>: <message>` line (50 at most), and then a count.
  - The boot log gives the first 16 warnings and the count (`configuration: N warning(s) for UNS hosts such as UMH Core`).
  - The web UI shows the warnings after a validation or a save, and marks the fields in amber.
  - With `sparkplug.naming: "umh"` (the UMH preset), `gw_config_parse()` refuses the first name finding, with its JSON path. A `primary_host_id`, a `type` and a `bytes` tag stay warnings: UMH cannot read them, but another host of the same broker can.
  - Choice (MAY): Sparkplug permits all these names (`tck-id-topic-structure-namespace-valid-device-id` and the metric name rules give only `/ + #` limits for the IDs). Thus the default behaviour accepts them, and only the preset that the user selects refuses them.
- **Device state with the UMH preset (#173).** With `sparkplug.naming: "umh"`, the NBIRTH has `Devices/<name>/Online` (Boolean, alias 80 + device index) for each device, and an NDATA gives each change. The value is the Modbus state of the device (MB-9): `true` also when its DBIRTH is too large for the broker (#76). The engine keeps one bit per device (`online_reported`, 4 B in `gw_node_t`: 21 488 B, still in the 21 504 B class of the heap, §1.3). The encoder makes the names of these metrics on demand in a scratch buffer of 64 B on the stack (`spb_payload_t.extra`), so there is no table of names and no allocation.
  - **NBIRTH size:** each of these metrics adds about 32 B plus the length of its name (`Devices/` + name + `/Online`). Thus 16 devices with names of 8 characters add approximately 0.6 KB, and 16 names of 39 characters add approximately 1.1 KB. An NDATA with one change has approximately 15 B of metric.
  - The gateway does not keep a change of the device state while the node is offline (store & forward, SPB-14): the next NBIRTH gives the current state.
  - Choice (MAY): the specification permits node metrics with any name. The preset puts the state of each device in a node metric, because UMH Core does not use the DDEATH (benthos-umh `sparkplug_b_input.go`).
- **Plain metrics with the UMH preset (#174).** UMH Core gives no value for a Template or a DataSet. With `sparkplug.naming: "umh"`:
  - a device with `type` publishes its tags as plain metrics with the tag aliases (100 + tag index), as a device without a type. The NBIRTH has no template definition, because no device is an instance (`tck-id-payloads-template-definition-nbirth` asks for a definition only for each instance). The validation still examines the `type` (the same tags in each device of a type), and gives no warning for it;
  - the NBIRTH and the diagnostic NDATA have no DataSet. They have the sums of the counters of all devices as UInt64 node metrics: `Node Info/Modbus/<counter>` (aliases 60–66) and `Node Info/Modbus TCP/<counter>` (aliases 70–74). UInt64, because a sum of 16 UInt32 counters can be larger than 2^32. The NDATA has them only after a change, as the DataSets;
  - choice: sums, not one metric for each device and counter. 16 devices × 12 counters would give 192 more metrics, and the NBIRTH would not fit the payload buffer without PSRAM (`CONFIG_GW_SPB_PAYLOAD_INTERNAL`, 6 KB). The counters of each device stay in the shell (`devices`), in `GET /api/status` and in the DataSets of the default behaviour. The state of each device is `Devices/<name>/Online` (#173);
  - the worst NBIRTH of the preset (16 devices with names of 39 characters, RSSI, Modbus TCP) is **2 917 B** with 67 metrics (`test_node`, `test_umh_worst_case_nbirth_fits`). It fits the 6 KB buffer. The node metrics of the preset come from the same array of the engine (51 metrics at most, `GW_NODE_METRICS_MAX` is 65), so the array of `gw_node_t` does not become larger;
  - the configuration hash of the store & forward ring includes `sparkplug.naming` (format 3), because a replay publishes a typed device as an instance or as plain metrics. A change of the preset starts a new ring. No gateway is installed, so there is no migration.
- **mDNS (NET-6).** The instance name is `<group_id>/<edge_node_id>`. Two IDs of 32 bytes give 65 bytes, but a DNS label has a maximum of 63 bytes, and `mdns_instance_name_set()` refused the name. Now the gateway cuts the name at 63 bytes, at the boundary of a UTF-8 character (`gw_config_mdns_instance()`).

### 6.3.1 Templates (SPB-18)

- **No template parameters or template version**, and no nested templates. A member cannot be a different template, and the gateway does not go into such a member if it arrives in a command. There are no templates for the node metrics (DI, DO, counters).
- A device with `type` does not publish one metric per tag now. A host that subscribed to `Meter1/Voltage` now sees `Meter1/<tipo>/Voltage` (Ignition: a UDT instance). This is optional and per device.
- The gateway does not use the aliases of the tags of a typed device on the wire (except the first alias, which is the alias of the instance). The gateway discards a DCMD by alias.
- The properties of the definition come from the first device of the type. The properties of each instance come from its own configuration.
- Nobody tested this with a real Sparkplug host (Ignition, Tahu). The tests used only the official proto and the rules of the 3.0 specification.

### 6.3.2 DataSet, Bytes and File (SPB-19)

- **Bytes and File: up to 64 B** per value (a fixed buffer on the stack of the codec). The gateway does not publish a longer value (the payload fails with a log entry). The gateway does not decode a command with such a value and discards all of the command. **File has no `metadata`** (name, size, md5), and the gateway does not split it into parts. There is no file transfer (REL-2 requires bounded memory).
- DataSet: the columns can only be numeric, Boolean, String or Text. They cannot be Bytes, DataSet or Template; the proto also does not accept these types in a cell. The gateway does not cut a cell text of more than 63 characters. The text makes the encoding of that message fail, with a log entry (issue #87). The only source, the device names, is shorter. At decode, the gateway gives the names and cells through callbacks and keeps the first 16 types.

### 6.4 Clock

**RTC (TIME-2).** At boot, before the configuration loads, the gateway reads the PCF85063 (0x51, on the same I²C bus as the TCA9554). The gateway uses the RTC time if three conditions are true. The OS bit (*oscillator stopped*) is clear. The time is valid (BCD, ranges, day of the month). The time is plausible (2025–2099). Then the gateway sets the system time. If not, the time stays unset until SNTP, as before. After **each** SNTP synchronization, the gateway writes the time to the RTC (this also clears the OS bit). The bus has one owner: `app_main` before `local_io` starts, and then that task. The task does the write request as it does the NCMD requests for the DOs.

- With the RTC time, the gateway does not wait the 3 s for SNTP (`CONFIG_GW_SNTP_WAIT_MS`). It does not block the TLS start either (it checks the certificate dates with that time). There is no *rebirth* when SNTP synchronizes later: the timestamps were already valid.
- **Without RTC, or with the oscillator stopped:** without SNTP there is no valid time. Before the first connection, the gateway waits up to 3 s for SNTP.
- The gateway does not consider the RTC time as synchronized (`Node Info/Time Synced`, TIME-3). The RTC drifts some seconds per day, and nothing corrects it until SNTP.
- **Compile-only on the chip:** the real I²C read and write of the PCF85063, and the 24-hour mode. The firmware assumes the reset value of the control register and does not change `CAP_SEL`. In QEMU, a simulated PCF85063 keeps the time in RTC memory. The time stays after a software restart and is lost with the process (OS bit at "power-on").
- In recovery mode and in commissioning, the gateway also reads the RTC at boot and writes it after SNTP (through the reduced `local_io` task of #74). We disabled the random start delay of the lwIP SNTP. In one of the runs, this delay moved the synchronization to after the NBIRTH.

### 6.5 Slow broker and command limits

- A slow broker blocks the Sparkplug task during each publish (each socket write waits up to 5 s, `network.timeout_ms`). The memory does not increase. While there is a session, the gateway **coalesces** the changes (each pass publishes the last value of each device). Store & forward (SPB-14) keeps only the data that occurs **without** a session.
- **Store & forward (SPB-14).** It keeps the data that DDATA/NDATA would send: tag values (with *deadband*) and DI/DO. It does not keep DBIRTH or DDEATH (the DBIRTH of the next session gives the state), or command responses. The gateway discards the samples of a device that is offline when the session comes back, because a DDATA without DBIRTH violates the protocol. The shell and the log count the discarded samples and the overwritten samples. The ring buffer is in RAM: a restart loses it. Only the samples on the microSD card (`store_forward.sd`) stay after a restart. With the spill on, a planned restart first moves the RAM ring to the card (issue #136, see "microSD: a planned restart"). The buffer keeps data from the first NBIRTH of the boot. It does not keep the data between the boot and the first session (the DBIRTH has the current value). Without SNTP and without RTC time (§6.4), the timestamps of the samples count from 1970, as the timestamps of the BIRTH messages do. The historical messages go out 4 per pass of the Sparkplug task, between the live data (the hosts identify them by `is_historical`). They use QoS 0, as all the other messages. Thus a cut during the send loses the data that esp-mqtt did not write yet. The data that stayed in the buffer goes out in the next session. 40 000 samples are approximately 11 h of a tag that changes each second, or approximately 2 min of 256 tags that change in each cycle.
- > **WARNING — microSD and GPIO45 (SPB-14, `store_forward.sd`).** Before you enable this function on a gateway, do one of these two steps. Examine the schematic: D0 must not have a pull-up, and the card must not be able to keep D0 high at reset. Or **set VDD_SPI by eFuse** in the factory process (`espefuse --chip esp32s3 set-flash-voltage 3.3V`, **irreversible**: after this, the chip does not read GPIO45 as *strapping*). If you do not, the 3.3 V flash of the module can fail to boot. The card uses SDMMC in 1-bit mode: CLK GPIO48, CMD GPIO47, **D0 GPIO45**. GPIO45 is the *strapping* pin of **VDD_SPI**. If it is high at reset (a pull-up of the card or of the socket on D0), the ROM supplies **1.8 V** to the flash. Nobody could verify this on the schematic or on a board. For this reason, the function is **off by default**, and when you enable it, the boot log shows a warning.
- **microSD: the card (issues #124 and #139).**
  - **Only for the gateway.** The product owner decided this on 7 Nov 2026. Do not put other files on the card. Format it with the gateway before the first use. The design does not support a card that holds other data (#138).
  - **Format.** The gateway uses FAT32 (FAT16 below 2 GB) with clusters of 32 KB. Industrial cards (pSLC) are recommended.
  - **Cards that do not mount.** These cards do not mount: a card with no file system, an exFAT card, an NTFS or ext4 card, and a card with a GPT partition table. ESP-IDF v6.1 sets `FF_FS_EXFAT` to 0 (exFAT needs long file names and has a licence question), and `FF_LBA64` to 0 (no GPT). Most cards of 64 GB or more come with exFAT. The boot does not format such a card. It writes the reason in the log, in the shell (`sdcard   NOT MOUNTED: …`) and in `/api/status` (`card.state` `not_mounted`, `card.detail`). Format these cards with the gateway.
  - **How to format (issues #139 and #137).** Use the web UI: the status page, the card "Tarjeta microSD", the button "Formatear tarjeta". The web UI asks you to type `FORMATEAR`. It tells you the number of other entries in the root of the card, if there are some. Or use the shell command `card format confirm`. Scripts can use `POST /api/card/format?confirm=1`, with the same Basic auth and CSRF check as the other writes.
  - > **CAUTION — Do not format a card that holds data that you need.** The format deletes everything on the card, also the samples that wait there for their publish.
  - **Never automatic.** The boot mounts the card with no format (no `format_if_mount_failed`). Only a request with a confirmation formats: the word `confirm` in the shell, the typed word and `?confirm=1` in the web UI. The format also operates when `store_forward.sd` is off, and on a card that did not mount.
  - **The format task.** The shell command and the web API only start the task `card_format`, and return at once (the web server never waits for the format). One format runs at a time: a second request gets `409` (busy). First, if the spill uses the card, the `sparkplug` task stops it between two card operations, closes its files and gives the card up (the RAM ring continues). Then the task stops the boot mount, starts the SDMMC host and the card itself, and writes zeros on the two GPT headers (sector 1 and the last sector). Then `f_mkfs` writes a new MBR with one partition and FAT32 with clusters of 32 KB (FAT16 below 2 GB). The data area starts at a multiple of 4 MB, the allocation unit of SDHC and SDXC cards, so each cluster and each extent of the ring is aligned to the erase blocks of the card. Last, the task mounts the new volume one time to check it.
  - **State.** The shell `status` (a `format` line, when a format was requested) and `card`, and `/api/status` (`card_format`), show `running` with the time, `done` with the FAT type, the cluster size, the size and the duration, or `failed` with the reason. After the format, restart the gateway to use the card. The web UI then shows its restart button.
  - **Refusals.** In QEMU (no card in the build): `501`, reason `no_support`, and nothing changes. Without PSRAM (the fault mode of #135): `501`, reason `no_psram`. A card that does not answer ends the format as `failed` (`no card answers (…)`), because the gateway can examine the card only in the task.
  - **The format needs the PSRAM.** The product owner decided this, as for the spill: the target board has PSRAM (#135). The task and its buffers use internal RAM only during the format (§1.2, §1.3).
  - **The spill needs the PSRAM.** The target board has 8 MB of octal PSRAM, and a board without PSRAM does not start the gateway (§1.3, #135). Only in the QEMU build without PSRAM, the spill does not start. The boot log and the shell (`spill    off: it needs PSRAM, and none was detected`) tell why, and the RAM ring overwrites its oldest samples when full.
- **microSD: the ring of blocks (issue #124).**
  - **Files.** The folder `/sf` holds the extents `r0000.sf`, `r0001.sf` and so on, and the two superblock copies `ringa.sf` and `ringb.sf`. All names are 8.3. An extent is min(4 MB, 128 clusters): 4 MB with clusters of 32 KB, 512 KB with clusters of 4 KB. Thus one FAT step that makes or deletes an extent changes a maximum of 128 clusters.
  - **Size.** `max_kb` is 128 MB by default (64 KB to 4 GB). The ring has a maximum of 1 024 extents: 4 GB with clusters of 32 KB, 512 MB with clusters of 4 KB. The default of 128 MB with clusters of 4 KB is 256 extents. The limit keeps a directory search to a maximum of 64 sector reads, and the boot list short. When the limit decreases `max_kb`, the boot log tells you to format the card with clusters of 32 KB.
  - **Block.** A block is 4 KB: a header of 32 B and a maximum of 169 samples. The header holds these items: the magic, the version, the sample size, the nonce of the ring, the configuration hash (see "microSD: the configuration hash"), the block number `seq`, the sample count, flags and a CRC-32C of the header and the samples. A string sample never goes into two blocks. The gateway writes a block one time, whole, at a position aligned to 4 KB, with no `fsync`. FatFs writes it directly to the card: there is no FAT or directory write in normal operation.
  - **Valid block.** The replay uses a block only if its CRC is correct, its nonce is the nonce of the ring, and `seq` modulo the ring size is its position. Thus the gateway never publishes a block that a power cut damaged, a block of an earlier ring, or a block of an earlier lap.
  - **Superblock.** Each copy holds a generation, the geometry, the nonce, the hash, the replay position (`tail`), a hint for the next block (`head`) and a CRC. The gateway writes the copy with the lower generation: one sector, with no `fsync`. It never writes the two copies less than 500 ms apart. It writes a new superblock (a new nonce or a new size) before any block that uses it. It writes a superblock after each 32 blocks of spill (the head hint; a quarter of the ring when the ring is smaller). It writes the head hint before the next block, so it also writes it when each pass of the loop writes a block (issue #142). It also writes one after each 64 KB of replay (the checkpoint).
  - **Other writes.** The gateway makes an extent at its first use (one step: `lseek` past the end and one `fsync`, a maximum of 128 clusters), and never changes its size after that. A power cut during this step loses a maximum of one extent of clusters. The gateway also makes the two superblock files one time. When it changes from one extent to the next, FatFs writes the directory entry of the closed file one time (its date).
  - **When the gateway writes.** The producers never write to the card. When the RAM ring is three quarters full, the `sparkplug` loop moves its oldest samples to the card, one block in each pass. Thus the gateway writes to the card only during a long outage.
  - **Full card.** When the ring is full, the next block replaces the oldest block. The gateway counts its samples as lost (`overwritten unsent`): it keeps the newest data. If the card is full before the gateway made all the extents, the ring stops at the extents that it has. It keeps the same nonce, so the data stays valid, and the boot log tells this. The ring keeps that size until a change of `max_kb` or a format.
- **microSD: boot and changes (issue #124).**
  - **Boot recovery.** The boot (`app_main`) only mounts the card. The `sparkplug` loop reads the card in steps after the network starts, or 10 s after the boot. It reads the two superblocks and lists the folder. Then it reads forward from the head hint while the blocks follow. It skips a maximum of 64 invalid blocks as holes (K = 64, 256 KB). Thus a normal boot reads a maximum of 32 + 64 blocks after the hint. Why 64: a power cut can damage NAND pages that the card programmed some pages before the cut. A block of 4 KB is smaller than a NAND page of 16 KB. One read takes 1 to 3 ms. A larger K cannot accept wrong data, because a block after a hole must have the expected `seq`. The replay does not start before the recovery ends, so the order stays correct.
  - **Holes.** The replay skips an invalid block between valid blocks and counts it as lost (`block(s) lost as holes`). It never publishes it.
  - **Both superblocks damaged.** The gateway reads the first block of each extent and takes the nonce that most valid blocks have. Then it finds the head with a scan. The replay position is lost: the gateway can send again all the data on the card, but it never sends damaged data. If no block is valid, it starts a new ring.
  - **A change of the configuration hash, or of `max_kb`.** The gateway writes a new superblock with a new nonce. All the old blocks are then invalid: the gateway does not publish the old data, and it deletes no file for it. When the ring becomes smaller, the gateway deletes the extra extents, one in each pass. The next item tells which configuration changes change the hash.
  - **The boot result (issue #152).** The log line `store & forward: card ready …` and the shell `spill` line tell what the boot recovery found. There are four results:
    - `new ring: no valid ring on the card`: no valid superblock and no valid block;
    - `new ring: another configuration or geometry`: a valid superblock of another configuration hash or another `max_kb` (or cluster size);
    - `ring found with its superblock`: the normal result after a restart;
    - `ring found again without a superblock (vote)`: both superblocks were damaged, and the vote found the ring. The gateway publishes its data again.

    After a new ring, the gateway does not publish the old data. Before issue #152, the log line showed `(new ring)` also after the vote, although the data was found again.
- **microSD: the configuration hash (issue #145).**
  - **What it is for.** A sample on the card holds a device index, a tag index and a value. After a restart, the replay uses the configuration of that boot to make a metric from the sample. Thus the samples are correct only if the indexes and the value encoding have the same meaning. The ring keeps a hash of the fields that set this meaning. If the hash at boot is different, the gateway starts a new ring.
  - **The fields in the hash.** `sparkplug.group_id` and `edge_node_id` (the topic of each replayed message). For each device, in order: `name` (the `device_id`), `type` (the template) and its range of tags. For each tag, in order: `name`, the Sparkplug datatype and, for a `bytes` tag, `length`. The datatype comes from `type`, `scale` and `offset` (§6 of the SPEC). It also sets how the replay reads the value of a sample.
  - **Changes that start a new ring.** A device or a tag that you add, remove, rename or move. A change of a datatype, for example `int16` to `uint16` without a scale, or a scale on an `int16` tag (Int16 to Float), or a scale on an `int32` or `int64` tag (Int32 or Int64 to Double, #165). A change of the template of a device, of `group_id` or of `edge_node_id`, and of the `length` of a `bytes` tag.
  - **Changes that keep the data.** The gateway keeps the stored value after the conversion and the scale, and the replay does not use these fields: `poll_ms`, `slow_poll_ms`, `scan`, `unit_id`, `offline_after`, `block_reads`, `max_gap`, `area`, `address`, `bit`, `word_order`, `deadband`, `writable`, `units`, `eng_low` and `eng_high`. Also a new value of `scale` or `offset` when the tag stays scaled (the datatype does not change), the `length` of a `string` tag (each sample holds its text and its length), and all other sections (network, MQTT, brokers, `rs485`, `local_io`, web). The samples of DI, DO and counters do not depend on the configuration.
  - **A firmware update keeps the data.** The gateway calculates the hash in `gw_core` (`gw_sfspill_cfg_hash()`, `gw_sfcfg.c`) from this explicit list of fields. Each field has a fixed size and byte order: little-endian integers, and each text with its length. The input starts with a format number (1). The hash never uses the bytes of a C structure. Thus a firmware that adds a field to `gw_tag_cfg_t` or `gw_device_cfg_t`, or that changes their padding, keeps the data on the card (upgrade and rollback).
  - **Before issue #145,** the hash covered the raw bytes of the `devices` and `tags` arrays. Thus each change of these structures, and each change of a field such as `deadband` or `poll_ms`, started a new ring. The first boot with the new hash also starts a new ring, and the samples on the card from the earlier firmware are not published. This is acceptable because the firmware has never run on real hardware: there is no installed base.
  - **The guard.** A new field in `gw_tag_cfg_t` or `gw_device_cfg_t` stops the build of `gw_core`, on the host and on the target. A positional initializer next to the list names each field, and `-Wextra -Werror` make a missing field an error. The comment there tells the developer to put the field in the hash or in the list of excluded fields. The guard counts fields, not bytes: it does not depend on the compiler, the padding or the size of an enum. The host test `sfcfg_guard` makes sure that the guard stops the build.
  - **Host tests.** `test_sfcfg` examines the excluded fields (the same hash), each field in the hash (a different hash), the order of devices and tags, and a golden value for a fixed configuration. It also writes the documented input byte for byte. `test_sfcfg_packed` runs the same suite with all structures packed and enums of 1 byte (other sizes and offsets), and gets the same golden value.
  - **Files of the earlier format (issues #122 and #151).** No gateway was installed with the format of step 1 (the segments `NNNNNNNN.sf`). Thus the firmware has no migration: a card with this format is reset. At boot, the gateway deletes these files and does not publish their data, also when they have the hash of the configuration. It truncates each file in steps of a maximum of 128 clusters, then it removes it. It never renames a file, because a power cut in a FAT rename can link two files to the same clusters. No step reads the file again, so a delete that a power cut stopped needs no mark: the next boot deletes the rest.
  - **The superblock layout of issue #151.** The superblock does not keep a position in the files of step 1 now: it is 64 B, not 72 B. A superblock of the earlier layout has a wrong CRC. Thus the gateway finds such a ring again by the vote of its blocks (see "Both superblocks damaged"), or it starts a new ring. There is no migration, because no gateway is installed.
  - **Files that are not ours (#134).** The gateway never touches a file in `/sf` with another name, or with a number at or above `0x80000000`. Such a file stays on the card.
- **microSD: what the historian receives (issue #124).**
  - **Duplicates after a restart.** The gateway saves the replay position on the card after each 64 KB of published history (2 730 samples). It saves it after the publish of those samples returned. Thus a restart or a power cut during a replay can send again a maximum of 64 KB of historical samples, approximately 2 700. If the power cut damages the save itself, the maximum is 128 KB. A planned restart (§"microSD: a planned restart") saves the position first. The duplicates have the same timestamps and values as the first copy. The host or historian must handle them: for example, keep one value for each tag and timestamp. A counter or an aggregate of the received messages counts them two times.
  - **Loss.** The history uses QoS 0: a power cut can also lose the messages that the TCP connection did not send yet. An unplanned restart (a power cut, a watchdog, a crash) loses the RAM ring: only the data on the card stays. A planned restart moves the RAM ring to the card first, in a time limit (§"microSD: a planned restart").
  - **Retention.** The gateway keeps only the changes (the RBE and `deadband` rules), not each poll. Thus calculate in changes per second. A change is one sample of 24 B (a text: 1 + length / 8 samples). A block holds 169 samples, so 1 MB of `max_kb` holds 43 264 samples. Hours ≈ `max_kb` × 0.01174 / (changes per second). The default of 128 MB holds approximately 31 h at 50 changes per second, and approximately 13 days at 5 changes per second. The RAM ring adds 40 000 samples. The `deadband` is the main control: set it a little above the noise of the sensor.
- **microSD: a planned restart (issue #136).**
  - **What it does.** Before a planned restart, the `sparkplug` task moves the samples of the RAM ring to the card. It moves the oldest samples first, in blocks, with one card operation in each step: a block, an extent allocation or a superblock. It feeds the watchdog after each step, as the normal spill does. Then it writes one superblock with the head and the replay position. After the boot, the recovery finds these blocks, and the replay publishes them one time, in order, before newer data. A sample never goes into two blocks, and never stays half in RAM.
  - **The planned restarts.** The shell `restart`, `POST /api/restart`, NCMD Node Control/Reboot, the restart after a firmware update (download or web upload), the restart after a saved configuration (web, shell), and the restart after the confirmation of a new image for the NVS encryption. All of them go through the `sparkplug` task, which owns the card. Thus no other task uses the card at the same time.
  - **A saved configuration (issue #141).** When the gateway saves a configuration, it calculates the configuration hash of the new configuration. Before the restart, it compares this hash with the hash of the ring. If they are the same (for example, a new broker address, a `deadband` or a `poll_ms`), it moves the RAM ring, and the next boot publishes the samples. If they are different (for example, a tag has a new name), the next boot starts a new ring and does not publish the old samples. Thus the gateway does not move the RAM ring before this restart, and the restart does not wait for the move. The log tells why: `the saved configuration has another configuration hash (<new>, the ring has <old>): the N samples of the RAM ring are not moved to the card`.
  - **Time limit: 5 s** (`FLUSH_LIMIT_MS` in `gw_edge.c`). The work is at most three quarters of the RAM ring of 40 000 samples: approximately 180 blocks, 2 extent allocations and 6 superblocks. At 1 to 5 ms for each block write (#124), this takes less than 1 s. The limit also permits 25 ms for each operation on average, or one FAT step of 1 s with the blocks. The watchdog does not set the limit, because the task feeds it after each operation. The operator who waits for the restart and the restart after a firmware update set it: the new image is already set to boot, and 5 s more do not change its trial. The gateway examines the limit before each operation, so the total time is the limit plus a maximum of one card operation. A superblock that must wait for the gap of 500 ms waits in the limit.
  - **When the limit expires,** the gateway restarts. The samples on the card are safe. The samples that stay in RAM are lost. The log tells the two counts (`the time limit of 5000 ms expired: N samples moved to the card, M samples stay in the RAM ring and are lost`). When all goes well, the log tells the samples, the records, the time, the blocks and the card operations.
  - **A card failure during the move** stops the move at once and stops the spill, as at any I/O failure (#134). The log tells the samples that moved and the samples that are lost. A block that the failure cut has a wrong CRC, so the replay never publishes it.
  - **No move.** The gateway does not move the RAM ring in these cases, and the restart loses it, as before:
    - the spill is off, or failed before (the log tells why);
    - the boot recovery did not end yet (the card is read after the network starts, or 10 s after the boot);
    - a format of the card was requested (#139: the format task waits for the card);
    - a factory reset with the BOOT button: the next boot runs another configuration, so the data could not be published;
    - a saved configuration with another configuration hash (issue #141): the next boot starts a new ring;
    - the rollback of a new image on trial (FW-1): the gateway judged the new image bad, so its restart does not depend on the tasks of that image. The rollback does not run in the `sparkplug` task;
    - an unplanned restart: a power cut, the task watchdog, a crash or a brownout.
  - **The `sparkplug` task does not answer.** The web server, the shell and the firmware update only set a request and return. If the `sparkplug` task does not start the restart in 20 s (`RESTART_ANSWER_MS`), the `esp_timer` task restarts the gateway without the move and without NDEATH. 20 s is more than one publish with its budget and its socket timeout (8 + 5 s), the longest normal pass of the loop. A task that hangs meets the task watchdog (15 s) first.
  - **QEMU (GW23, PSRAM variant).** The E2E stops the broker and changes the registers until the RAM ring holds 300 to 749 of its 1 000 samples (below three quarters: the spill does not start). Then it restarts with the shell `restart`, and one more time with `POST /api/restart`. Each time, the log shows the move, and after the boot the historical DDATA has exactly the moved samples: one time, in order, and nothing from before. Issue #145: then the E2E saves a configuration with `PUT /api/config`. It changes a `deadband`, the `units`, the `scale` of a scaled tag, `poll_ms` and `offline_after`. The boot log shows the same configuration hash, and the moved samples come back in the same way. Then the E2E saves a configuration that renames a tag. The gateway does not move the RAM ring before this restart, and the log line names the two hashes (#141). The boot log shows the new hash and a new ring, and no historical DDATA comes. The default variant (no PSRAM, no spill) does not change.
- **microSD: failures, tests and limits.**
  - **I/O failure (#134).** An I/O failure stops the spill until the next boot, and the RAM ring overwrites its oldest data again. The samples on the card are not counted as waiting: the shell shows them apart (`spill    FAILED (…): N samples stay on the card unsent`). Thus the `sparkplug` loop does not wake every 10 ms for data that it cannot replay. The next boot finds them again.
  - **Values for the board tests (issues #18 and #131).** The shell `status` shows a `card` line: the cluster size and, for each card operation (`open`, `block`, `super`, `read`, `alloc`, `step`, `stat`, `list`), the count, the average time and the maximum time. It also shows the errors and the longest time without a watchdog feed of the `sparkplug` task since the boot. The `spill` line shows the samples on the card, the samples lost (overwritten, holes), the blocks of the ring, the superblock writes and checkpoints, the most samples published between two checkpoints (the duplicates limit), the time, the block reads and the result of the boot recovery, and the deletes. The shell command `card` shows the `card` line, the `sdcard` line (the state of the card) and the `format` line.
  - **QEMU.** Mount, SDMMC, FAT and the format are compile-only: QEMU has no SDMMC. The E2E (`GW23`) tests what QEMU can run: the refusal of the format (`501`, and nothing changes), the auth and CSRF checks of `POST /api/card/format`, the `card`, `spill` and `card_format` objects of `/api/status`, and the microSD section of the page. The emulator-only shell command `card format simulate <ms>` runs the format task with no write: the same release of the spill, the same states and the same `409`. The E2E uses a LittleFS directory (`CONFIG_GW_SF_SPILL_LITTLEFS`) with a "cluster" of 32 B: each extent is one block of 4 KB, so the E2E makes 32 extents for `max_kb` 128. LittleFS needs an `fsync` after each write, the card does not. The QEMU PSRAM build has a RAM ring of 1 000 samples (`sdkconfig.qemu-psram`), so that a short outage spills.
  - **What remains for the board (#131).**
    - the format from the web UI (#139): a card with no file system, an exFAT card of 64 GB or more, an NTFS card and a GPT card. After the restart, the spill starts. Also the duration of the format on cards of 32, 64 and 128 GB, the free stack of `card_format` (the log line at its end), and that the `sparkplug` task and the web UI continue to operate during the format (#137);
    - the time of each operation on 3 card classes, with clusters of 32 KB and of 4 KB: block write, superblock write, extent allocation on an almost full card, boot recovery;
    - a soak with a broker outage of 24 h;
    - at least 200 power cuts for each card class, with a counter tag;
    - `fsck.vfat` on a PC at intervals.
  - **Residual risks.** When FatFs closes an extent, it writes its directory entry again. A power cut during that write can damage the directory sector, which also holds other entries. The blocks stay self-checking, but only the power cut tests of the board (#131) can show the effect on the file system. On an almost full or fragmented card, the first allocation of an extent can read a large part of the FAT. #131 measures it.
- **Watchdog with a slow link (issue #59).** The Sparkplug task feeds the TWDT after each publish. Thus a rebirth (NBIRTH and up to 16 DBIRTH) can take more than 15 s without a restart of the gateway. But one publish had no limit: esp-mqtt continues to write while each write makes progress. Now each publish has a budget (`CONFIG_GW_MQTT_PUBLISH_BUDGET_MS`, 8 s). When the budget expires, a timer does a `shutdown` of the socket. The current write fails, and esp-mqtt closes the connection (`publish of N bytes to … still running after 8000 ms: link too slow for a Sparkplug session`). The session then tries again with *backoff*. The total limit is the budget plus the *timeout* of the current write (5 s): 13 s, below the 15 s of the TWDT. Such a link cannot keep a Sparkplug session. The retry cuts again at the same large DBIRTH, and the gateway stays without a session but does not restart. The timer uses the local port to check that the descriptor is still the descriptor of that connection. After each NBIRTH, the log shows the duration of the births and the longest interval without a watchdog feed.
- **E2E of the slow link.** A relay of the harness reads the data from the gateway at 700 B/s. slirp and the host sockets keep more than 100 KB before the write of the gateway waits. Thus the harness requests *rebirths* until these buffers are full. At 700 B/s and with payloads of a maximum of 6 KB, the E2E does not reach the 8 s budget reliably. Thus the QEMU build decreases the budget to 2 s (`sdkconfig.qemu`). The E2E runs the budget cut, the session close and the return with the fast link. It does not run the TWDT trigger itself (QEMU runs with `wdt_disable`).
- **"Session up" only with the births out (issue #71).** If the connection drops immediately after the SUBACK, the gateway does not start the births (`SUBACK received but the connection is already gone`). If the NBIRTH or a DBIRTH does not go out, the gateway does not consider the session up (`NBIRTH not published` / `births incomplete`). The next session then sends all the births again. An NBIRTH that went into the socket immediately before the cut can still be lost on the network: the host resolves this with the bdSeq of the next session.
- The gateway discards commands of more than 1024 bytes, and commands that esp-mqtt fragments, with a log entry.
- The gateway discards **all** of an NCMD or DCMD that nanopb cannot fully decode (for example, a name of more than 63 characters or a string of more than 256). The log tells the reason (`string overflow`) and the limits. The gateway decodes the command one time for a check and one more time to apply it.
- The write queue to the bus has 8 positions (`CONFIG_GW_BUS_WRITE_QUEUE_LEN`). If **all** the writes of a DCMD do not fit, the gateway discards **all** of the DCMD, with a log entry (`DCMD for 'Meter1': 3 writes but the bus queue has room for 2: nothing applied`). The gateway never applies a DCMD only partially (#60). Unknown or read-only metrics do not count: the gateway discards them alone, as before.

### 6.5.1 TLS to the broker (MQTT-4, MQTT-5)

- **No clock, no TLS.** With TLS, the connection waits for a valid time: the RTC time at boot (§6.4) or SNTP. The log shows `TLS: waiting for SNTP before connecting`. There is no 3 s limit, as in the case without TLS. Without a valid time, each certificate would be "not valid yet". Without RTC time and without a reachable NTP server, the gateway does not connect by TLS. If you do not want a date check, compile with `CONFIG_MBEDTLS_HAVE_TIME_DATE=n`. Then the gateway also does not reject expired certificates.
- **The certificate files go into LittleFS next to `config.json`** (the `fs/` folder at flash time, `idf.py flash`). A file change applies at the next restart. The secrets migration of CFG-7 erases the full partition, but it keeps these files (it copies them to RAM and writes them again).
- **Memory.** A TLS session uses approximately 40 KB of mbedTLS heap. The custom allocator (`CONFIG_MBEDTLS_CUSTOM_MEM_ALLOC`, in `gw_storage_init`) uses PSRAM, and not internal RAM when the PSRAM is full (#135). It uses internal RAM only in the fault mode (its HTTPS server) and in the QEMU build without PSRAM. In PSRAM without flash encryption (FW-3), these buffers are in plain text outside the chip.
- The public CA bundle is the common subset of ESP-IDF (`CONFIG_MBEDTLS_CERTIFICATE_BUNDLE_DEFAULT_CMN`). It is compile-only: the E2E uses its own CA.
- **The private key of the client (MQTT-5) is in plain text in the flash** in the default build, in LittleFS. It is also in RAM while the firmware runs. The key cannot go to the encrypted NVS (CFG-7), because esp-mqtt needs all of the key in memory. Flash encryption (FW-3) is not in `main` (§3.7). Anyone who reads the flash gets the key. The gateway does not accept keys with a password (encrypted PEM).
- **DS (Digital Signature) peripheral of the S3: out of scope.** With DS, the RSA key is never in plain text. But DS needs an HMAC key burned in an eFuse block, and the project burns no eFuse (issue #133, §3.7).
- **Cause of a certificate rejection (issue #70).** esp-tls reports the verification *flags* only if the certificate of the broker is still available after the failure. With TLS 1.3, it is not available (upstream report in `docs/upstream-reports.md`). The firmware installs its own mbedTLS verification callback through the `crt_bundle_attach` hook of esp-tls. This callback uses the CA of the file, or chains to the callback of the public CA bundle. It keeps the *flags*, the *subject*, the SANs and the dates of the certificate. The log tells the cause (`expired`, `not valid yet`, `does not carry the expected name`, `not signed by the configured CA`). A second log line gives the certificate, the expected name, the time of the gateway and the SNTP synchronization state: `TLS: certificate CN=broker.e2e [IP:10.0.2.2,DNS:broker.e2e], valid … to …; expected name 'wrong.e2e' (mqtt.tls.server_name); clock … UTC, SNTP synchronised`.
- **Chain to the CA bundle without private symbols (issue #77).** The verification of the public CA bundle is a function of `esp_crt_bundle.c` without a public declaration. The firmware does not use the name of this function. It calls the public API `esp_crt_bundle_attach()`, reads the callback that this API installed in the mbedTLS configuration, and calls it from its own callback. If a future IDF gives the function a new name, nothing changes. If the callback type or the field changes, the build fails with a message that gives the reason (`_Static_assert`). If `esp_crt_bundle_attach()` does not install a callback any more, the attempt fails with an error in the log. The gateway never accepts a certificate without verification. The supported versions are in §3.1.
- The handshake runs in the esp-mqtt task, not in the Sparkplug task, so it does not affect the TWDT of the Sparkplug task. After each NBIRTH, the log shows the stack margin of the two tasks (`stack free (high-water mark): sparkplug … mqtt_task …`).

### 6.5.2 Broker list (MQTT-6)

- **Policy for the return to the primary broker.** By default (`primary_return_s: 0`), the gateway keeps a backup broker that operates correctly. Each broker change is a new session (NDEATH, NBIRTH, all DBIRTH), and the Sparkplug hosts see it. With `primary_return_s` > 0, each return causes a short cut, also if the primary broker is still down (one attempt, which fails, and a return to the backup broker). The gateway cannot probe the primary broker without a cut of the session.
- The credentials, the `client_id`, TLS and the CA are the same for all brokers.
- The brokers do not share state: a command in transit on the old broker is lost. A primary host (SPB-13) must connect to all brokers.
- **Primary host offline (issue #156).** A valid offline STATE is not a failed attempt. The gateway goes to the next broker at once and ignores `failover_after` (`tck-id-operational-behavior-edge-node-termination-host-offline-reconnect`). If the host is offline on all brokers, the gateway goes from one broker to the next, each time after the minimum backoff. This does not count for the "no broker reachable" state of the interface (NET-4), because the network operates.
- A host with a number of DNS records (for example, a cluster) is still one broker: esp-mqtt uses the first address that it resolves.

### 6.5.3 MQTT 5 (MQTT-8)

- The gateway uses only the MQTT 5 functions that Sparkplug needs. These are the connection properties that the specification sets (above) and the reason codes of CONNACK and SUBACK (≥ 0x80 = rejection, as in 3.1.1). The gateway does not use topic aliases, message expiry, user properties or shared subscriptions.
- **DISCONNECT with the reason code 0x04 (issue #155).** With MQTT 5, each DISCONNECT of the gateway has the reason code 0x04, *Disconnect with Will Message* (`tck-id-payloads-ndeath-will-message-publisher-disconnect-mqtt50`). This applies to an NCMD Reboot, a restart, a change of broker or interface, and a session that the gateway closes. Thus the broker also publishes the NDEATH of the *will*. After an explicit NDEATH, the hosts get two NDEATH messages with the same bdSeq. A Sparkplug host accepts this: the second NDEATH has no new data. When the gateway closes a session before the NBIRTH, the hosts get an NDEATH with a bdSeq that no NBIRTH had, and they ignore it. esp-mqtt sends the DISCONNECT from `esp_mqtt_client_stop()`, `esp_mqtt_client_destroy()` and its task after `esp_mqtt_client_disconnect()`, and clears the disconnect property after each DISCONNECT. Thus `gw_edge` sets the property again before each of these calls.
- `CONFIG_MQTT_PROTOCOL_5` adds code to the image also when the configuration uses 3.1.1 (measured in §3.2). Without this option, the gateway reports a configuration with `"5"` and does not connect.
- **Maximum Packet Size (issue #73).** The gateway obeys the limit that the broker announces in the CONNACK. It does not send a larger message (`…/DBIRTH/GW26/Bulk: 2489 bytes, more than the broker's Maximum Packet Size of 2000 (by 489): not published`, a maximum of one time per minute in the log). The shell shows it (`limit    broker Maximum Packet Size 2000 B; N message(s) too large, not published (last: <topic>)`). A DBIRTH that does not go out leaves its device unborn (no DDATA). The host does not see that device until you make its tags smaller or increase the limit. Since issue #76, the gateway does not build that DBIRTH again at each polling cycle. It tries one time per session (the limit can change with the next session) and one more time at each *rebirth*. The configuration changes only with a restart, which is a new session. An NBIRTH that does not fit closes the session, as a rejected SUBSCRIBE does. The gateway does not read the *Receive Maximum* (the publishes are QoS 0).

### 6.6 Network: Wi-Fi, failover (NET-4) and IPv6 (NET-7)

- **Broker reachability (issue #57): the gateway measures it with its own attempts, not with a probe.** Sometimes the cable stays connected, but the Ethernet router does not route to the broker any more. Then the failover occurs only when **all** brokers in the list fail, `failover_after` times each. With one broker and the default values, this time is approximately 20–30 s. It is the keepalive time until the session drops, plus 3 attempts with *backoff* of 1, 2 and 4 s and up to 5 s of connection *timeout* each. The gateway does not probe the failed interface while the session uses the other interface. When the wait expires, the session goes back to the failed interface (one more NDEATH and NBIRTH). If the path is still broken, the session drops again after a new round of attempts, and the wait doubles (60 s, 2, 4… up to 30 min). With one interface, there is no alternative and nothing changes. Host tests only: QEMU has no Wi-Fi.
- **esp-mqtt 1.1.0 leaks memory with `network.if_name` (issue #58).** `esp_mqtt_set_config()` (`mqtt_client.c:633`) does a `calloc` of a new `if_name` at each call and does not free the old one (approximately 17 B per connection attempt). The same occurs with `credentials.authentication.key_password`. The firmware gives the interface binding only to `esp_mqtt_client_init`. Each `set_config` goes through a wrapper. The wrapper rejects (and in debug aborts) a configuration that still has these fields. The host test `check_set_config` fails if somebody calls `esp_mqtt_set_config()` directly again. **Upstream report pending:** this environment cannot open issues in espressif/esp-mqtt. The text of the report is in `docs/upstream-reports.md` §1. Remove the wrapper when a version corrects the leak.
- **Boot with the two interfaces:** if Wi-Fi gets an IP before Ethernet (the W5500 needs 1–3 s to negotiate), the first session uses Wi-Fi. The session goes back to Ethernet after `failback_s`. Thus there is one extra session at boot (one NDEATH and one more NBIRTH).
- **Interface change when the old interface is dead:** the explicit NDEATH does not arrive. The broker publishes the *will* only when its keepalive expires (1.5 × `keepalive_s`). The *will* has the bdSeq of the old session and can arrive after the new NBIRTH. A Sparkplug host discards it by bdSeq. The same occurs if the broker publishes the *will* when the new client (same client_id) takes the session.
- `esp_mqtt_client_destroy` can block the Sparkplug task up to the network timeout (5 s) if the client was in a connection attempt. This stays below the 15 s watchdog.
- The firmware does not copy the Wi-Fi credentials to the NVS of the driver (`WIFI_STORAGE_RAM`). The password is in the secrets of the encrypted NVS (CFG-7), not in `config.json`.
- The Wi-Fi reconnection uses backoff with jitter from 1 to 30 s. The radio power save is disabled (`WIFI_PS_NONE`) because of the command latency.
- **Nobody tested MQTT over IPv6 end to end.** slirp forwards the connections to `fec0::2` to the `::1` of the host, and the kernel of the test container has no IPv6 (`AF_INET6` not supported). The E2E tests the addresses (link-local, SLAAC, static) and the ICMPv6 reachability of the virtual slirp host. esp-mqtt resolves the host with `getaddrinfo` and accepts IPv6 literals. SNTP and DNS over IPv6 are compile-only.
- IPv6: there is no DHCPv6 and no static *gateway*. The router comes from the RAs. Without RAs, a static address can only reach its /64. The SLAAC address uses EUI-64 (it shows the MAC; lwIP has no temporary addresses). The static address is only for Ethernet. The shell shows the addresses that the gateway saw since the last link up. lwIP does not tell when an address expires.
- The Wi-Fi static IP has the same rules as the Ethernet static IP. With the two interfaces on the same subnet, the traffic that is not MQTT (SNTP, DNS) goes out through the interface that lwIP selects. MQTT is bound to the active interface.

### 6.7 Remote syslog (REL-4)

- **UDP only, no acknowledgment.** The gateway does not send again a datagram that the network lost. The sender gives the `sequenceId` its number at send time, so a gap on the server is a network loss. The lines that the gateway discarded arrive as a notice `N log line(s) not forwarded`.
- **There are no logs over MQTT.** They would put non-Sparkplug traffic into the session of the node and into the publish queue. Syslog continues to operate when the broker is down, and that is when the log is most necessary.
- The gateway discards a line if a different task formats a line at the same moment (the lock does not wait). It also discards a line if the ring is full (no network, server not reachable). The gateway truncates a line of more than 256 bytes, and the message to 199 characters.
- The gateway does not forward the lines from before the configuration loads (boot, restart reason), because the destination is in the configuration.
- No TLS (RFC 5425). The gateway resolves the server when it opens the socket, only when an interface is active, and again after a send error. If the name does not resolve, the gateway tries again each 5 s.
- The lines of `ESP_EARLY_LOG` and the lines from interrupts do not go through `esp_log_set_vprintf`. They stay only on the console.

### 6.8 Persistence and aliases

- The bdSeq is not in NVS (issue #161): it starts at 0 at each boot, and a connection attempt writes nothing to the flash.
- The aliases depend on the order of the tags in the configuration.
- `Node Control/Rebirth` has no alias in the NBIRTH (`tck-id-operational-behavior-data-commands-rebirth-name-aliases`, #153). Thus each host sends the rebirth request by name. The alias 2 stays reserved. The gateway also accepts an NCMD with the alias 2 as a rebirth request.

### 6.9 DI and DO

- The gateway loses a DI change that is faster than the debounce, and its pulse does not count (IO-4). Without PCNT, the counter sees up to approximately 25 Hz with a 20 ms debounce.
- **Counters (IO-4):** a power cut, a *panic* or the watchdog loses the count since the last save (a maximum of `counter_save_s`, 10 min by default). More frequent saves wear the flash. With 60 s, there are approximately 1 440 writes of 64 B per day: decades with the NVS *wear leveling*. There is no NCMD to set the counters to 0. The factory reset erases them with NVS. A controlled restart saves them in the *shutdown handler*, except after a factory reset (NVS does not accept state writes then).
- The NDATA timestamp is the timestamp of the batch of changes, not of each bit.
- **DO boot state (IO-3).** At each boot (cold, warm, by watchdog or by panic), **before the configuration loads**, the DOs go to OFF. The exceptions are the `hold` DOs of the last valid configuration. The gateway saves that mask in NVS when it accepts a configuration, and reads it at boot. Thus NVS initializes before the outputs. The TCA9554 does not reset with the ESP32 and keeps its latch. For this reason, the firmware reads CONFIG and OUTPUT and writes the latch again before it sets the pins to output. The latch value is OFF, or the kept level of a `hold` DO whose pin was already an output. The firmware also sets the polarity register (0x02) to 0. The expander keeps this register, and a value other than 0 from a different firmware would invert the read-back. With a valid configuration, the `on` DOs go on. With a rejected configuration or in commissioning, the `hold` DOs also go to OFF. Until then, they kept their state for the time of the configuration load: some tenths of a second, or some seconds if a secrets migration occurs.
- **A `hold` change applies from the boot after the boot that loaded it:** the first step of the boot uses the mask of the previous configuration.
- **Safe state on MQTT loss:** the Sparkplug session (NBIRTH published) counts, not the link. The safe state triggers one time per cut. When the session comes back, the DOs do not go back to their previous state automatically. An NCMD cannot arrive without a session, so there is no conflict with it. The safe state does not change the DOs of the local rules (IO-7).
- **Expander that does not respond.** If the TCA9554 does not answer at boot (for example, an I²C glitch), the gateway publishes the DOs as **`is_null`** in the NBIRTH. The latch can keep outputs on from before a warm restart, so the gateway never reports them as off without knowledge. The gateway rejects the NCMDs on them. The `local_io` task tries the initialization again at 1, 2, 4… up to 60 s. When the expander responds, the DOs get their boot state (IO-3), and an NDATA publishes their 8 values. The real I²C is compile-only. In QEMU, a RAM model of the TCA9554 runs the retry (E2E #74), and host tests examine the logic. **Also in recovery mode and in commissioning** (#74): there, only the part of the `local_io` task that tries the expander again (and writes the RTC) starts. Its target is all DOs OFF, also if the latch kept a DO on from before a warm restart. The shell (`status`) and `/api/status` (`io.do` = `null`, `do_unknown`, `expander`) show the state and the attempts.
- **Local rules (IO-7):** the only logic is DO = DI, DO = not DI and toggle on edge. There are no timers, no combinations of a number of DIs and no rules to the Modbus slaves. You cannot command a DO with a rule by NCMD, and such a DO has no safe state on MQTT loss.
